### Frankfurt and Leipzig, 1784.

<summary>This title page introduces a collection of Masonic orations published anonymously in 1784, intended for members of "authentic" Masonry. It highlights the Enlightenment-era focus on the search for truth and the official sanctioning of the text by Masonic leaders.</summary>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page is the verso (back side) of the title page. The bleed-through mentioned in the source indicates the physical transparency of the 18th-century paper, showing the title and 1784 date from the front. This page functions as an epigraph, setting the philosophical tone for the Masonic speeches that follow.</meta>

->There is still much between heaven and earth, of which<-
->our entire philosophy lets us dream<-
->nothing at all.<- <note>original: "Es ist noch viel zwischen Himmel und Erden, das von sich unsere ganze Philosophie gar nichts träumen läßt." This is a German translation of Hamlet’s famous line to Horatio: "There are more things in heaven and earth, Horatio, than are dreamt of in your philosophy." In the 18th century, this quote was frequently used by those interested in the "higher" mysteries of Masonry to suggest that rationalist science could not explain everything in the universe.</note>

->Shakespeare in Hamlet.<-

<image-desc>A small engraved or woodcut vignette depicting two young children or cherubs sitting together on a grassy patch in a landscape setting.</image-desc>

<summary>This page presents a famous quote from Shakespeare’s Hamlet as a motto for the book, suggesting that the Masonic content within explores mysteries beyond the reach of conventional philosophy. It reflects the 18th-century German fascination with Shakespeare as a herald of deep, natural truths.</summary>

<keywords>Shakespeare, Hamlet, philosophy, mysticism, 18th century, Enlightenment, epigraph, German literature</keywords>

## A Brief Preliminary Reminder.

It has indeed become customary to publish nothing, no matter how small, without adding a preface, introduction, or preliminary reminder, regardless of whether the subject matter itself requires it. <note>In the 18th century, it was a point of etiquette for authors to apologize for publishing their work, often framing it as a service to the public rather than an act of vanity.</note> One might quite rightly reproach me for doing so now, as such a thing is certainly not necessary for these few short speeches. However, since I have a humble request to present to my esteemed readers, I cannot refrain from adding this preface. I shall be as brief as possible, so as not to try your patience or your kind indulgence.

I ask my honored readers, both within and outside the <term>Order<gloss>Orden—the fraternal organization of Freemasons.</gloss></term>, to keep in mind that the differences in these speeches—regarding both their subjects and their style—are a result of the various locations where they were delivered, as well as the specific relationship they were intended to have with the audience itself. One had to be instructional, another encouraging and cautionary <note>original: "vermahnend"—to give a serious warning or moral exhortation.</note>, while yet another was merely

<meta>catchword: Bes</meta>

<summary>The author provides a brief preface to his collection of speeches, explaining that their varying tones and subjects were tailored to specific Masonic audiences and locations. He adopts a humble tone, adhering to the 18th-century literary convention of justifying one's publication to the reader.</summary>
<keywords>preface, Masonic speeches, 18th-century etiquette, rhetoric, fraternal order, audience adaptation</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues from page XI, where the author was explaining the different purposes of his speeches. The previous page ended with the catchword "Bes," which leads into the word "Beweise" (evidence or proof) at the start of this page.</meta>

provide evidence. Besides this, they were delivered at very different times, and would likely never have been shared with the public if various brothers had not deemed these speeches worthy of their approval and persuaded the author, through frequent requests, to publish them in print. This was done partly to strengthen younger brothers in their zeal for <term>Freemasonry<gloss>Maurerei—the principles and practices of the Masonic fraternity.</gloss></term>, and partly to share with kind readers outside the <term>Order<gloss>Orden—the Masonic organization.</gloss></term> some concept of its noble pursuits, and thereby to somewhat diminish the preconceived ill opinion that some among them have adopted toward it.

Kind readers outside the Order! Allow me, therefore, without overstepping my duties, and without wishing to persuade you to join the Order yourselves, to briefly touch upon the <term>main grievances<gloss>original: "Haupt-Gravamina"—a Latin legal term referring to the most serious complaints or burdens of proof brought against a party.</gloss></term> that are forced upon it, and to answer them impartially.

First, it is said: the entire purpose of a rational human being is well known; it consists in the glorification of the Creator through the fulfillment of our duties. Our duties are determined by philosophical and Christian morality, and are in relation either to God, to ourselves,

<meta>catchword: selbst,</meta>

<summary>The author explains that he published his speeches to inspire younger Freemasons and to correct public misconceptions about the Order. He begins to address common criticisms by discussing the shared human duty to the Creator and the foundations of morality.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, public opinion, morality, duties to God, grievances, Enlightenment philosophy, fraternal order</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues from page XII. The previous page ended with the author listing the categories of human duty: to God, to ourselves, and (continuing here) to our fellow humans. The author is addressing the specific criticism that Freemasonry is redundant because it overlaps with existing religious morality.</meta>

ourselves, or our fellow humans. <term>Freemasonry<gloss>Maurerei—the principles and practices of the Masonic fraternity.</gloss></term> cannot determine duties more important than those of <term>Christian morality<gloss>christliche Moral—the ethical framework based on Christian teachings which served as the primary moral standard for European society during this period.</gloss></term>, or if it does determine such duties, they are superfluous and useless; therefore, the whole of Freemasonry is something superfluous and useless.

Here, nothing has yet been proven to me. The duties toward one’s neighbor and oneself are very extensive; and granted that Freemasonry provides one with a more certain and better-grounded knowledge of one’s own worth in regard to our present and future <term>vocation<gloss>Bestimmung—the philosophical concept of a divinely intended purpose or destiny for a human life.</gloss></term>, and provides the means by which a physical and moral benefit arises for our <term>fellow humans<gloss>Nebenmenschen—literally "near-people," referring to those around us or our neighbors in a moral and social sense.</gloss></term>, through which one perfects oneself and thereby glorifies the <term>Creator<gloss>Schöpfer—referring to God as the architect or maker of the universe.</gloss></term>; then Freemasonry would indeed always be a laudable and highly useful object of our occupation. Besides this, however, morality limits itself merely to the determination of those duties necessary for a person to help achieve their <term>bliss<gloss>Glückseligkeit—a state of supreme happiness and spiritual fulfillment, often cited in Enlightenment philosophy as the ultimate goal of human existence.</gloss></term>, and provides rules for the application of these to the various circumstances of each individual. Consequently, we not only value it immensely, but it is the foundation of all our teachings. Since, however, our pursuits are not merely focused on the knowledge of our duties—

<meta>catchword: ge-</meta>

<summary>The author refutes the claim that Freemasonry is useless by arguing that while it is built upon the foundation of Christian morality, it offers a more profound understanding of one's purpose and practical means for self-improvement and service to others.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Christian morality, duty, self-perfection, Enlightenment, vocation, bliss, the Creator</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues from page XIII. The previous page ended with a discussion of human duties and whether the ethical framework of Freemasonry is redundant in light of existing religious morality. The sentence concludes here, arguing that if Masonry facilitates a deeper understanding of God and the natural world, it cannot be considered superfluous.</meta>

directed, but rather that we primarily apply ourselves to the <term>closer knowledge of God<gloss>nähern Erkenntniß Gottes—the pursuit of a more intimate or detailed understanding of the Divine through reason and study, a hallmark of Enlightenment-era theology.</gloss></term> through the means granted to us for that purpose, and that the contemplation of nature and the works of creation, combined with the strictest observation of moral duties, is incumbent upon us; so I ask: whether (—) <term>Freemasonry<gloss>Maurerei—the practice of the Masonic craft and its philosophical tenets.</gloss></term> is something useless or superfluous (given these circumstances)?

Secondly, it is said: Why are you so secret! It is envious to withhold true knowledge, and a fraud for those you admit if you possess none.

Just as there is knowledge that would be superfluous, indeed perhaps dangerous, to a child, yet is absolutely necessary for a man, so it is with knowledge that has a moral reference. The most magnificent and perfect knowledge produces in the head and heart of a villain and immoral person the material for new and never-before-invented crimes. <note>The author argues that advanced philosophical or "secret" knowledge requires a foundation of virtue, otherwise it becomes a tool for harm.</note> Regarding this, I ask every upright person whether we do well or not, in the current sad decay of humanity, to close our doors to the <term>vicious<gloss>Lasterhaften—those who are prone to vice or lack moral integrity.</gloss></term>; and let the upright, worthy man step forward for whom they were ever closed! The latter,

<vocab>Knowledge of God, Creation, moral duties, Freemasonry, Secret, humanity, the vicious</vocab>

<meta>catchword: teres, (the beginning of "letzteres" on the following page)</meta>

<summary>The author defends Freemasonry against charges of redundancy and secrecy, arguing that its practices lead to a deeper understanding of God and that its secrets are protected only to prevent their misuse by the immoral.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, secrecy, moral education, knowledge of God, vice vs virtue, Enlightenment philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of the Order’s secrecy and membership selection from page XIV. The previous page ended by discussing how doors are opened only to upright, worthy men whose character acts as a safeguard against the misuse of knowledge.</meta>

honesty, I think, guarantees against all fraud. That we have, unfortunately, despite all caution and effort, been deceived; sad experience proves that. However, it strengthens us even more in our once-formed resolution. <note>The author acknowledges that despite their vetting process, unworthy men have occasionally gained entry to the Order, but argues this failure only reinforces the need for strict standards.</note>

Thirdly, it is objected: there are many **fanatics** among you. — Can true philosophy and fanaticism ever be combined! The former is and will always remain the basis of all our teachings, and the <term>fanatics<gloss>Schwärmer—in the 18th century, this term was a sharp pejorative for those who followed irrational impulses, mystical delusions, or religious hysteria rather than reason.</gloss></term> in our **Order** show by that very fact that they are certainly distancing themselves from its true principles, and therefore provide evidence for us, rather than against us. Even the most magnificent and wholesome teachings of religion have produced fanatics, but for that reason, no one has thought to deny religion itself or to consider it dangerous. No, one always praised and revered the cause and the teaching, and pitied the unfortunate soul who, from a subject that alone would have been capable of providing him with peace, gathered the material for the most sorrowful unrest.

Therefore, kind readers, do not accuse us too harshly; and although much may appear to be against us, do not confuse those who instill this suspicion in you with the inward, sincere <term>seekers of truth<gloss>Wahrheitsforscher—literally "truth-researchers"; individuals dedicated to the systematic and honest pursuit of wisdom.</gloss></term>

<summary>The author defends the Masonic Order against the presence of "fanatics" (Schwärmer), arguing that their irrationality contradicts the Order's philosophical foundations just as religious extremists contradict the purity of faith. He acknowledges that while the Order is sometimes deceived by newcomers, this only proves the necessity of their secretive and cautious admission process.</summary>

<keywords>fanaticism vs philosophy, deception, religious analogy, seekers of truth, defense of the Order, Schwärmerei, moral integrity</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of the Order against common accusations. The previous page ended with a distinction between irrational "fanatics" and sincere "seekers of truth." This page concludes the third point of defense and addresses political loyalty before transitioning to a preface for the speeches that follow.</meta>

searchers and admirers, who seek to promote their happiness through the glorification of their Creator and the application of their gathered knowledge for the welfare and benefit of their fellow human beings and themselves, amidst the noise and bustle of this world, in silence and solitude.

I have merely intended to touch upon the three aforementioned points, as they are the most significant accusations burdened upon us; the others seem far too insignificant to me for a response to be necessary. Even the idea frequently held against us—that we wish to form a <term>state within a state<gloss>original Latin: "statum in statu." This was a common 18th-century political charge against secret societies, accusing them of forming a private government that bypassed the laws of the land.</gloss></term>—is in itself so ridiculous that one would waste time dwelling on it. Before God, we can testify that the words of our divine Master: **Render unto Caesar what is Caesar’s** <term>etc.<note>original: "2c." (et cetera). A reference to Matthew 22:21, emphasizing political obedience.</note></term> and **be subject to the authorities** <term>etc.<note>A reference to Romans 13:1, a standard biblical defense of a citizen's duty to the state.</note></term> are written far too deeply in our hearts for us to tolerate even the thought of acting against them.

Furthermore, I ask my esteemed readers for kind indulgence regarding the following speeches. They are intended merely to be feelings of the heart, not a warehouse of well-chosen, artificial rhetorical phrases; and I also believe for certain that the former (at all times) lose something when combined with the latter (—). For the harmony of both in a way,

<summary>The author concludes a defense of the Order by dismissing the "state within a state" accusation as ridiculous and affirming the members' religious and civic loyalty. He then introduces the following speeches as heartfelt expressions rather than polished rhetoric.</summary>
<keywords>loyalty to the state, biblical obedience, Caesar, state within a state, sincerity vs. rhetoric, silence and solitude</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s introductory remarks regarding the emotional and spiritual intent of his speeches. ...that the **intellect** <note>original: "Verstand." In the 18th century, this specifically referred to the faculty of logical reasoning.</note> might be delighted by them, and the **heart** most deeply moved, would be perfection; and we finite creatures are not destined to achieve this. Meanwhile, in order to show my readers for what reasons I have often been moved to speak a word of **truth** to the **brothers** <note>This refers to fellow members of the author's secret society or fraternal order.</note> entrusted to me, I consider it my duty to point out something here to the praise and honor of a worthy man.

I, as well as several of my dear brothers, read with deep feeling—and I may say, without looking too much at the story and the outer shell, but all the more at the essence—the "little Voltaire" <note>The author is likely referring to one of Voltaire’s philosophical tales, such as *Zadig* or *Micromégas*. It is notable that he finds "religion" in Voltaire, who was often criticized by the Church as an atheist.</note> (indeed a **novel**; but would that they were all like this!) and were most deeply moved by the excellent **morality** contained within it, and by the hints that always lead back to **religion** and the **fear of God**. This sparked the thought in me to make use of the then-current **state of mind** of my dear brothers, and I therefore composed the speech on the **Fear of the Lord**. If it has awakened any benefit among my good brothers, or should it produce any in my other readers, let it be attributed solely to that good and excellent **writer** who gave my heart the direction to immerse myself entirely in the fear of the Lord,

### Some Freemasonic

# Assembly Speeches,

->published<-

->by<-

### a devoted admirer and sincere seeker of the truth and fellow-member of genuine masonry <note>The author uses the phrase "genuine masonry" (original: *ächten Maurerei*) to distinguish his practices from other contemporary secret societies or "irregular" lodges that were common in the late 18th century.</note>.

# Contents.

**I.** Address of the Master to his Brothers.
**II.** Speech on the fear of the Lord <note>original: *Furcht des Herrn*. This refers to a foundational Masonic and biblical concept of profound reverence for the Divine, whom Masons often call the Great Architect of the Universe.</note>.
**III.** Speech on the genuine occupation of a Mason.
**IV.** Speech on nature and the necessary knowledge thereof.
**V.** Speech on the necessary connection of religion with Masonry.
**VI.** Speech given at the installation of the Lodge F. to the T. in B. <note>original: *L. F. z. T. z. B.* In 18th-century German Masonry, these abbreviations often identified specific lodges; this likely refers to the Lodge "Friedrich zum Tempel" in the city of Braunschweig (Brunswick).</note>
**VII.** Speech given at the turn of the year, December 31, 1783.

# Foreword.

If in this speech mention is frequently made of the true **Light of the World**, spread over all nature and flowing from the supreme perfection of the Creator, it could very easily be that many of my kind readers would not understand me. The true, genuine **Mason** <term>Mason: A member of the Freemasons, a fraternal organization that uses the tools of stonemasonry as symbols for moral and spiritual self-improvement</term> certainly understands me; the novice will one day perceive it through God's gracious understanding, if he remains faithful to his duties and his calling, and obedient to the **Order**. At this point I might say: *a word to the wise is sufficient* <note>original Latin: "sapienti sat"</note>. Meanwhile, I believe I will make this subject in the speech itself even more clear—

<summary>In this preface, the author introduces the central concept of the "Light of the World" and acknowledges that while it may be mysterious to the uninitiated, it will be understood by dedicated members of the Masonic Order.</summary>

# All-esteemed Brothers!

Many call themselves Masons <term>Masons: Members of the Freemasons, a fraternal society that uses the tools and language of stonemasonry as metaphors for moral and spiritual development</term> without even knowing the true, great value of their title, and this for no other reason than because they do not understand the actual purpose of Masonry. Many are not lazy in their efforts, but rather strive to find the traces of truth; however, they do not succeed in their endeavor because the true—and indeed only—means by which they can reach their ultimate goal are not known to them.

Masonry has only one purpose, and there are only a few specific means—and no others—to achieve it. Firmly convinced of this great truth, I have resolved to offer you, my brothers, today a short instruction based on the beautiful words of the Gospel of St. John, Chapter 1, verse 5 <note>original Latin: "Evangelii St. Johannis Cap. I. v. 5"</note>: “The light shines in the darkness, and the darkness has not understood it.” <note>This biblical passage is a cornerstone of Masonic ritual, often interpreted as the pursuit of divine or intellectual enlightenment amidst a world of ignorance.</note>

von <note>Catchword: "of" or "from," likely continuing into a phrase such as "from the..." on the following page.</note>

<summary>The author begins a formal address to his fellow Freemasons, arguing that many members fail to reach the "truth" because they misunderstand the organization's singular purpose and the specific means required to achieve it. He introduces a short instruction based on the Gospel of John regarding light and darkness.</summary>

Many will object to me: Why do I not recognize the holy Light of the World? Since it produces such glorious and healing effects, it should rightfully be recognized by the whole world; because a benevolent Creator would certainly not withhold his treasures of grace from anyone. But this language belongs to the fools, who speak of wisdom without knowing it. The merciful Creator invites the whole human race to participate in his treasures, but he also says: seek, and you shall find <note>A reference to the Gospel of Matthew 7:7: "Seek, and ye shall find."</note>. And this is where the main point lies: devotion to the will of God, combined with the most sincere diligence, helps everyone to attain their ultimate purpose.

[Through the] <note>The start of the sentence is missing from the previous page, but the context implies "Through the..."</note> Fall of Man, nature has been placed into that sad and dark state in which it unfortunately finds itself at present, where one sees how death, the wages of sin <note>A reference to Romans 6:23: "For the wages of sin is death."</note>, tears everything away after the disruption of its parts, and nothing appears again. This can already be explained entirely in accordance with Nature. If we closely observe our teacher, Nature, we will find that she always proceeds by degrees. The most imperfect can gradually attain the highest degree of perfection of which it is capable. Man, as a child and as a man, is always the same being, even though he gradually attains the degree of his perfect physical development. If we find ourselves in this life, according to our activities, in the process of rising further in our blessedness, then we have the certain confidence that we shall attain from one level of perfection <note>The author views "perfection" not as a finished state, but as a series of progressive stages or "degrees."</note> to another in that [future] life, since death and decay <note>original: "Verwesung"</note> are merely means of purification for our presently corrupted nature. For although his body dies, unfortunately, the earthly, sinful inclination toward vice <note>original: "Laster"</note> does not die with it. Even if we were permitted to believe that all the earthly parts clinging to him could consume one another after a certain time, and he, thus purified from them, could draw some hope of rest through the immeasurable grace of God—which one would have to regard as the first stage of a kind of blessedness for him—it would still be for a thinking [being...] <note>The sentence ends abruptly at the bottom of the page.</note>

Only those who are of a pure heart, who have a firm and grounded trust in God, and who abstain from all fleshly earthly desires, can hope—through the grace of the Triune Being <note>Referring to the Holy Trinity, though presented here as a universal spiritual requirement for Masonic advancement.</note>—to penetrate into the sanctuary, and thus achieve the true purpose of all Masonry <note>original: "Maurerey".</note>, which consists merely in the zealous endeavor to reach the <term>Knowledge (Erkenntniß): In this context, it refers to "Gnosis" or a deep, experiential spiritual understanding rather than just intellectual facts.</term> of this almighty, fundamentally good Creator Himself through the knowledge of the true Light of the world spread across all of nature—as an <term>Outflow (Ausfluß): An emanation; a concept where all reality is seen as flowing from a single divine source.</term> from the highest perfection of God. This purpose is achieved solely and only through the following means to be indicated in my second part.

There are only three of them which alone are effective.

True zeal in the investigation of nature, according to that most excellent rule: recognize God by His works. <note>A hallmark of Enlightenment-era "Natural Theology," which argued that God's existence and attributes could be proven through the study of the natural world.</note>

->Sincere trust in God, and finally<-

->Diligent prayer; these three elements <note>The three elements are: 1. Zealous investigation of nature, 2. Trust in God, and 3. Prayer.</note> must always be bound together.<-

Every investigation must necessarily be appropriate to that which one wishes to examine and learn to recognize: It is therefore essential that a true <term>Natural Philosopher (Naturforscher): In the 18th century, this term described someone who studied the physical world to uncover spiritual and divine truths.</term> submit himself to the primary laws of nature. These laws consist, however, in the fact that nature proceeds in all things <term>Gradually (stufenweise): This refers to the "Great Chain of Being," an ancient and medieval concept where all of creation exists on a continuous ladder from the simplest minerals to the highest spiritual beings.</term>. If he wishes to recognize nature perfectly and soar to the highest peak—namely, to the <term>Knowledge (Erkenntniß): Here meaning "Gnosis," or a profound, experiential spiritual insight rather than mere intellectual facts.</term> of the <term>All-Enlivening Light (allbelebendes Licht): A term used in esoteric traditions to describe the divine life-force or the spiritual sun that sustains all existence.</term>—he must begin with the study of the lowliest part of nature and ascend through the gradations established and found within it, up to man and the higher <term>Entities (Wesenheiten): This likely refers to spiritual beings, such as angels or celestial intelligences, believed to exist between humanity and the Divine.</term> linked with him.

Only then will he work in the right manner and become capable of even higher insights, of whose reality I am indeed certain and most inwardly moved and convinced; however, my dearest friends, I am far too incapable of describing and explaining them to you. All these labors presuppose another highly important task, without which the former would never succeed. It is the working upon oneself <note>A core principle of Freemasonry and alchemy, symbolizing the purification of the soul—often described as smoothing a "rough ashlar" or stone.</note> as a consequence of self-knowledge; the latter, however, requires the most precise examination of oneself.

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual development. The previous page (14) concluded the three essential means for achieving knowledge: diligent investigation of nature, trust in God, and prayer. This page begins a new section focusing on the internal process of "Self-examination" (Selbstprüfung), which follows the external and divine preparations previously mentioned. <page-num>15</page-num>

Nothing is more necessary, and nothing more difficult in my eyes, than self-examination. It requires a degree of sincerity that should rightly be found in every person, especially in a <term>Mason (Maurer): A member of the Masonic fraternity, for whom "Know Thyself" is a primary command for moral and spiritual progress.</term>, but unfortunately it occurs in only very few. Many people believe that self-examination consists merely of remembering one’s gross <term>Sins of action (Thatsünden): Overt physical acts or "actual sins," as opposed to internal thoughts or inclinations.</term> and perhaps showing some remorse for them. This, however, is only the lowest degree of self-examination; for it also searches the most hidden corners of the thoughts and senses of the heart, and testifies not only to earnest remorse for the sins committed—whether they be small or large, or perhaps seem quite insignificant—but it also moves one to make the firm resolution to renounce them entirely, to combat one's passions, and to begin a <note>The phrase "Gott wohlgefälligen Wandel" implies a total transformation of one's lifestyle and conduct to be in harmony with divine will.</note> life pleasing to God.

Should someone have the good fortune to be able to examine himself in such a way that he, as if an impartial judge, allows all his actions and most hidden thoughts to appear before him and judges them, he will certainly attain the purpose of <term>Self-knowledge (Selbsterkenntniß): In this context, a mystical and moral clarity where the individual sees their true spiritual state without the delusions of the ego.</term>. He, however, has achieved it who is conscious of all his faults and inclinations, and who is sincerely concerned with separating the sinful and the earthly from them. To attain this goal...

->Sincere trust in God, and finally<-

->Diligent prayer, which three parts must always be bound together.<-

Every investigation must necessarily be appropriate to that which one wishes to examine and learn to recognize: It is therefore essential that a true <term>natural philosopher (Naturforscher): In the 18th century, a "Naturforscher" was not just a scientist in the modern sense, but someone who studied the physical world to find spiritual truths.</term> submit himself to the fundamental laws of nature. This consists, however, in the fact that nature proceeds by degrees in everything. If he wishes to recognize her perfectly and soar to the highest summit—namely, to the <note>The "knowledge of the all-vivifying light" (Erkenntniß des allbelebenden Lichts) refers to the ultimate spiritual enlightenment or the divine source behind all creation.</note> knowledge of the all-vivifying light—then he must begin with the study of the most insignificant part of nature, and through the <term>gradations (Gradationen): The "Great Chain of Being" or the idea that nature is organized in a continuous ladder from simple matter to humans and then to spiritual beings.</term> established and found within it, rise up to man and the higher essences connected with him. 

Only then will he work in the right way and become capable of even higher insights, of whose reality I am indeed certain and most deeply moved and convinced, yet, dearest friends, I am far too incapable of describing and explaining them to you. All these labors presuppose another highly important task, without which the former would never succeed. It is the <note>The "working upon oneself" (Bearbeitung seiner selbst) is a core Masonic metaphor, often compared to a stonemason smoothing a rough stone to make it fit for a temple.</note> working upon oneself as a consequence of self-knowledge; the latter, however, requires the most precise examination of oneself.

Nothing is more necessary, and nothing more difficult in my eyes, than **self-examination**. It requires a degree of **sincerity** which should rightfully be found in every human being, especially in a <term>Mason (Maurer): A member of the Freemasons. In this period, Masonic philosophy heavily emphasized "polishing the rough stone" of one's character through rigorous moral self-improvement.</term>, but unfortunately occurs in only very few. Many people believe that self-examination consists merely of remembering one’s gross <term>sins of commission (Thatsünden): Sins involving specific outward actions or deeds, as opposed to "sins of omission" or internal thoughts.</term>, and perhaps showing some **remorse** for them. This, however, is only the lowest degree of self-examination; for it also searches the most hidden corners of the thoughts and senses of the heart. It does not only testify to earnest remorse for sins committed—whether they be large or small, or perhaps appearing quite insignificant—but it further motivates one to form the firm resolution to renounce them entirely, to combat one's **passions**, and to begin a way of life pleasing to God.

Should one have the fortune to be able to examine oneself in such a way that, as an impartial judge, as it were, one allows all their actions and most hidden thoughts to appear before them and be judged, then they will certainly achieve the purpose of **self-knowledge**. But he has reached it who is aware of all his faults and inclinations, and who is sincerely concerned with separating the sinful and the earthly from them. To reach this goal—

## Most dearly beloved brothers all!

If any subject has particularly occupied mankind at all times, it is certainly the desire to enlarge oneself <note>original: "Begierde sich zu vergrößern." In this context, it refers to the human drive for self-improvement, growth, or perhaps social status</note>; and had man only remained within the true, correct concept of his actual greatness, he would have also achieved the purpose of his creation. For God created man to make him happy—that is, to enlarge him—inasmuch as the happiness or greatness of man undoubtedly consists in recognizing his <term>inner worth<gloss>the inherent moral and spiritual value of an individual independent of external status</gloss></term> and the application of that value. 

How greatly, alas, do people at present misjudge their true inner worth, and how often do impure intentions still determine many actions that appear good to us; actions which, if we were to judge them by their underlying motives, would only leave us with even sadder and more distressing impressions of the decay of the human race. We want to believe, for the honor of humanity, that prejudices of the intellect have more part in the

<summary>This page serves as an introductory address to a fraternal group, discussing the divine purpose of human "greatness" as the recognition and application of one's inner moral worth. The author laments the current state of humanity, where seemingly good deeds are often driven by impure motives or intellectual prejudices.</summary>

<keywords>inner worth, human greatness, divine creation, morality, fraternal address, Enlightenment thought, human decay</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the thought from page 27, completing the sentence regarding how "prejudices of the intellect" contribute to the confusion between true and apparent greatness.</meta>

confusion of true and apparent greatness, rather than perhaps a corruption of the heart—although in many, the heart has already sunk deep into ruin. Unfortunately, man commits the error of deceiving himself all too often. He mistakes the appearance for the essence, chases after will-o'-the-wisps, and loses sight of the bright light he ought to follow. This happens not only from a lack of proper <term>judgment<gloss>original: "Beurtheilungskraft," the faculty of discerning truth from falsehood</gloss></term>, but also because deceptive appearances make a greater impression on his outer sensuality and cause a stimulation that produces many momentary comforts. This constantly misleads him to pursue these very things; from this, habit arises, habit becomes his second nature, and the poor soul rushes toward his own destruction with rapid strides. This, in short, is the consequence of misjudging one’s own worth. However, determining that worth is not difficult; let us examine it a little more closely.

How do we judge the actual inner value of objects outside of ourselves? Only by whether they are fit for their <term>purpose<gloss>original: "Bestimmung." This term refers to one's "vocation" or the divinely intended goal for which a human or object was created</gloss></term> or not. From these objects, we need only draw conclusions back to ourselves. Man must therefore know his purpose in order to see if he can find within himself the means to become capable of fulfilling it.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the true worth of a human being, answering the question posed at the end of the previous page regarding how one determines their "Bestimmung" or divinely intended purpose.</meta>

What then is the <term>vocation of man<gloss>original: "Bestimmung des Menschen," a central theme in 18th-century philosophy concerning the purpose for which God created humanity</gloss></term>? — Reason and religion prove to us convincingly that it is solely the glorification of the Creator through the fulfillment of the duties incumbent upon us. This is the true and only purpose of mankind, and a greater or lesser fitness and ability for it determines a person’s inner worth and greatness.

Given this recognized truth, my dearest ones, who could still make any claim to real greatness? For a glance directed at ourselves convinces us most certainly that a goal stands before us to be reached, while there is only weakness and <term>impotence<gloss>original: "Ohnmacht," meaning a total lack of power or capacity to act independently of divine grace</gloss></term> on our part. Only the **will** lies within us, and even this is all the merciful Creator demands, since all strength and the accomplishment itself can be obtained from Him alone. <note>This reflects a common Pietist or devotional sentiment: that human effort is limited to the desire to do good, while the actual power to do good is a gift from God.</note> 

Therefore, the closer a person approaches their true greatness, the smaller they seem to themselves, and the more they scorn the greatness of this world. This worldly greatness can only be a hindrance to achieving one’s actual purpose—all the more so since it is, unfortunately, built upon <term>self-conceit<gloss>original: "Eigendünkel"</gloss></term>, prejudice, ambition, misfortune, and the ruins of humanity.

Therefore, always consider, O mortal! that you are and remain a powerless being; keep Him ever before your eyes through whom alone you are capable of anything, and especially apply yourself with

<summary>The author defines the true purpose of human life as glorifying God through duty and argues that true greatness is found in humility rather than worldly ambition. He asserts that while humans have the will to do good, the strength to achieve it comes solely from the Creator.</summary>

<keywords>vocation of man, glorification of the creator, duty, humility, impotence, worldly greatness, self-conceit, divine grace</keywords>

But through what means—given the insight into one's own powerlessness and weakness, which does not permit one to reach any purpose through one's own strength—how can one obtain the necessary support of their merciful Creator? Only through love and voluntary submission to His highest will, or to put it briefly, through the **fear of the Lord**.

This fear of the Lord, which brings such supreme happiness to mankind, shall be, my dearest ones, the subject of the present short reflection, for which I ask your kind attention.

### I. The Importance of the Fear of the Lord, and
### II. The Fruits Thereof.

When a person, turned inward and filled with the deepest feeling of love for their Creator, casts a glance at the course of our present world, they must necessarily feel the utmost sorrow when they see how illicit desires—which, unfortunately, already dominate mankind enough—are satisfied without any restraint; how virtue, which alone can satisfy a person, is not only no longer taken into consideration, but it is considered a matter of honor to mock both it and the most sacred truths of a religion that only seeks to promote human happiness. When, upon thrones and under the <term>cloak of philosophy<gloss>original: "Deckmantel der Philosophie," likely a critique of radical Enlightenment thinkers who the author believes use "reason" to undermine faith</gloss></term>, truths are laughed at, and errors take their place in the minds of otherwise great geniuses—who, if they applied the magnificent gifts of the spirit bestowed upon them by <term>Providence<gloss>original: "Vorsehung"</gloss></term> to their true intended purpose, would not fail to bring happiness to humanity and themselves; but in the aforementioned manner, they become the scourge of mankind and turn their talents into weapons against themselves. When among lesser <term>geniuses<gloss>original: "Genies," referring here to individuals of high intellectual or creative ability</gloss></term> such principles, out of imitation, the fashion of the day, the tickle of...

At this, the heart of every person devoted to the good bleeds—one who would so gladly communicate their views and feelings to the general mass, but unfortunately finds an insurmountable resistance in this regard. Such a person can only seek, as much as lies in their power, to do good for themselves and must strive to come ever closer to perfection, guided by the hand of a few faithful friends who are joined to them either by duty, mutual advantage, or conscience.

# Respected and worthy brothers all!

A Mason <term>Maurer; a member of the Freemasonic fraternity</term> who is proud of his knowledge proves that he does not possess the actually necessary and true knowledge of our holy Order; for in this case, he would have to confess countless times that our knowledge is merely piece-work <note>original: "Stückwerk." A biblical allusion to 1 Corinthians 13:9, "For we know in part," emphasizing that human understanding is incomplete.</note>, and that he must daily implore the Giver of all good: "Give me first enlightened eyes of my understanding, so that I may recognize what is pleasing to Thee." In this short but very emphatic prayer, the true knowledge of the Mason is contained; for he who, through devotion to the will of God, has attained enlightened eyes of his understanding to recognize what is pleasing to God, alone possesses true, unerring knowledge. However, this would be of no use to him if he did not allow it to pass into activity <note>The speaker highlights a core Masonic principle: that spiritual or philosophical insight is worthless unless it results in charitable or productive "work" in the physical world.</note> with the intent of doing the best for his fellow men, the perfection of himself, and the glorification of his Creator. Consequently, most worthy brothers, I leave it to your impartial consideration,

**Mason** <gloss>Maurer</gloss>, **Order** <gloss>Orden</gloss>, **Knowledge/Insights** <gloss>Kenntnisse</gloss>, **Prayer** <gloss>Gebeth</gloss>, **Devotion** <gloss>Ergebenheit</gloss>, **Fellow men** <gloss>Nebenmenschen</gloss>, **Perfection** <gloss>Vervollkommnung</gloss>, **Creator** <gloss>Schöpfer</gloss>

I shall therefore, with you, most worthy brothers,

### engage in reflections on the true work and genuine efforts of a thinking and searching Mason.

Even if the true Mason views himself merely as a natural man, and reflects only upon that moment when his intellect <term>Verstand; the capacity for rational thought</term> reached maturity—namely, when he became capable of inferring the existence of objects outside himself through the sensations of his senses—what might his first thought have been upon beholding an object? None other than the desire to learn what kind of object it might be, for what use it was intended, and who had crafted it. Just as easily as he expresses this desire regarding objects found outside himself, and which, merely because they stand in connection with him, or a—

<summary>The orator argues that the true goal of Freemasonry is the moral improvement of the heart rather than the mere intellectual study of ancient symbols. He begins a philosophical exploration of how a "natural man" uses his senses and reason to understand the world and its Creator.</summary>
<keywords>Masonic work, heart vs intellect, moral improvement, natural reason, purpose of existence, Hieroglyphs</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's philosophical inquiry into the nature of the "thinking Mason." The opening syllable "nen" completes the German indefinite article "einen" (a/an) from the previous page, referring to the "precise influence" objects have on a human being.</meta>

[page 55]
[sig] D 4 [/sig]

precise influence and connection to him, instilling in him the drive to want to know them; just as easily, and even more likely, he will and must express this desire toward himself. And from this, three questions will quite naturally arise: Who am I? To what <term>Endzweck; ultimate purpose or final goal</term> am I destined? And who made me? — Three important questions, the answering of which provides a great deal of material for our labor.

Who am I? A creature of the All-Benevolent and the All-Wise, who—through the combined powers of my soul, spirit, and body <note>The speaker utilizes the traditional tripartite division of the human being: the soul (Seele), the spirit or intellect (Geist), and the physical body (Körper).</note>—is able to infer <note>original: "schließen"; to conclude or reason logically</note> from my own nature the existence of other spiritual and physical beings outside myself, and consequently can work toward the knowledge of them and the glorification of the Creator. May this thought of everything that can raise us weak and wretched humans to some degree of dignity remain forever imprinted in all our hearts with indelible letters!

To what am I destined? If it is true that we, with the combined powers of soul, spirit, and body, recognize other things outside ourselves and are permitted to draw conclusions from them about ourselves, then it certainly remains our primary purpose and duty to investigate <note>The text ends mid-word with "nachzufors," completed by the catchword "fors" as "nachzuforschen" (to investigate or research).</note> all these things as much as lies within our power—

### Very Worshipful <term>Master in the Chair</term> <note>original: "Mstr. v. St." (Meister vom Stuhl). This is the presiding officer of a Masonic lodge.</note>

### All present, most beloved and worthy Brethren!

Today is one of those fortunate days for me in which I find a renewed opportunity to thank Providence that access to our sacred teachings has been granted to me. I enjoy the happiness of being permitted to appear before a society that, with united strength, devotes itself to virtue and integrity; a society that removes all the shimmer and splendor of the earth from the true essence of man, viewing him only as he appears in his nakedness <note>In this context, "nakedness" refers to a person's character stripped of social titles, wealth, or worldly rank.</note>, and judging and evaluating him by that standard alone. How particularly I must rejoice that, even though I do not belong to your specific <term>Observance</term> <note>original: "Observanz." In the 18th century, Freemasonry was divided into different "Observances" or systems of rites, such as the "Strict Observance."</note>, you have nonetheless invited me to attend your <term>labors</term> <note>original: "Arbeiten." In fraternal traditions, "labors" or "work" refers to the performance of rituals and moral instruction.</note>.

This is immensely flattering for me. I feel it most vividly, and I offer you...

Today is one of the fortunate days for me, in which I find a new opportunity to thank <term>Providence<note>German: "Vorsicht." In 18th-century Masonic and Enlightenment discourse, this refers to a divine or guiding spiritual force, often called the "Great Architect of the Universe" by Masons.</note></term> that I have been granted access to our <term>sacred teachings<note>German: "geheiligte Lehren." A central Masonic concept focusing on moral excellence and the improvement of the self.</note></term> and integrity. This society removes all the shimmer and glamour of the earth from the actual essence of man, considering him only as he appears in his <note>original: "Blöße." This refers to the Masonic concept of the "naked" man—stripping away social status, wealth, and titles to judge a person solely on their character.</note> nakedness, and judging and assessing him only according to that. How particularly I must rejoice that, even though I am not of your <term>Observance<note>German: "Observanz." This refers to a specific system or "Rite" of Masonry. In the 18th century, there were various competing systems, such as the "Strict Observance." The speaker is noting that he belongs to a different branch of the fraternity.</note></term>, you have nonetheless invited me to attend your <term>labors<note>German: "Arbeiten." A technical term for Masonic meetings and the performance of rituals.</note></term>.

This is uncommonly flattering for me. I feel it most vividly, and render you

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech from the previous page, where the guest speaker was addressing the Masonic lodge. The previous page ended with the beginning of a formal expression of gratitude.</meta>

...render you my most devoted thanks; and the pure brotherly love that I cherish for you awakens in me an ever-stronger, sincere wish to soon have the happiness of seeing brothers united who perfectly agree regarding their goal, and whose differences may consist, at most, only in words. 

However, I will move on from this and content myself with saying: we are all Masons—that is, seekers of **Truth** and <term>practical Christians<note>In the 18th century, "practical Christianity" referred to a focus on living out Christian ethics and performing "good works" in the world, often contrasted with a focus on rigid theological dogmas or church rituals.</note></term>. For far be it from those who, through ignoble deeds, dishonor the high name they are unworthy to bear. 

But happy—a thousand times happy—is he who, as a genuine seeker of pure truth, has always directed his gaze toward great, glorious Nature. In her, as in a mirror, he perceives with every step of further knowledge the finger and the influence of the <term>triune Being<note>German: "dreieiniges Wesen." A reference to the Trinity, but here framed through the lens of "Natural Theology," where God's nature is discovered through the study of the physical world.</note></term> within her. And finally, after the elevation of his <term>spiritual self<note>German: "Geistmensch." A term used in German mysticism and esoteric thought to describe the inner, eternal spirit of a person as opposed to their physical, "fleshly" body.</note></term> and the removal of all that is earthly and carnal, he learns to act within Nature himself. She, in turn, reacts back through constant <term>action and reaction<note>German: "Action und Reaction." The speaker is applying the language of Newtonian physics to spiritual life, suggesting a reciprocal, energetic relationship between the human soul and the natural world.</note></term>. Through this, combined with the influence of God, a threefold influence upon one another arises, creating an excellent and inexpressible harmony. This harmony serves the Mason’s highest and sole happiness, the honor and praise of God, and the benefit of his fellow man.

When we observe Nature with attention, we find in her so much incentive for the fulfillment of our duties, out of gratitude toward the Highest, that it seems impossible to us to believe a true <term>naturalist<note>German: "Naturforscher." In the 18th century, this term described anyone who studied the physical world—encompassing what we now call biology, chemistry, and physics—often with the goal of understanding the Creator's design.</note></term> could be anything other than a truly <term>practical Christian</term> at the same time. Therefore, dearest brothers, let our first diligence be directed—before we engage in a laborious exploration and collection of certain knowledge—toward preparing our inner selves perfectly, to recognize and improve it perfectly, but especially to work upon our hearts so that they may become perfectly receptive to the Truth; consequently, to banish everything raw and wild from them, and instead to let true love, as the genuine character of sincere disciples of a divine master, rule therein, and through this to impart true sensitivity to the heart. 

This <term>sensitivity<note>German: "Empfindsamkeit." A major 18th-century cultural movement that emphasized deep, sincere emotion and moral empathy as a path to virtue.</note></term> is very different from, and entirely opposed to, weak <term>sentimentality<note>German: "Empfindelei." A derogatory term used by Enlightenment thinkers to describe shallow, self-indulgent emotionalism that leads to no useful action.</note></term>. Only the strong man is capable of the former; though he is moved to the highest degree by the beauty of Nature and the grace of the Creator constantly manifesting therein, he is nonetheless in that same moment intent upon the application of this knowledge. Sentimentality, by contrast, is merely the result of a sensation occurring in weak nerves; it ends with that sensation and possesses no power either for action, nor for the...

# Most Serene, Most Worshipful Grand Master.
<note>The term "Most Serene" (Durchlauchtiger) was a formal address for high-ranking royalty, while "Most Worshipful" (Hochwürdigster) is the specific Masonic honorific for a Grand Master.</note>

## Dear Brothers, worthy of all veneration!

Every thinking being must necessarily adopt certain principles as a guiding rule for his actions as soon as his intellect reaches maturity. For a person living merely in a state of nature, these principles consist either of certain self-prescribed duties—which, through the harmony of his reason and inner sentiment, seem necessary for achieving and promoting his true happiness—or in the intention to reach one or more ultimate goals appropriate to each person's character by observing certain <term>rules of prudence</term><note>original: "Klugheitsregeln." In 18th-century philosophy, these are practical guidelines for making wise choices to avoid harm, as opposed to absolute moral laws.</note> through which harm and disadvantage are prevented.

It would be difficult to find a person endowed with reason who has not conceived of certain principles of this kind. Everything would therefore depend only on investigating what ultimate goal these duties or rules of prudence had as their basis? I firmly believe
<note>The text ends here on the word "believe" (fest), matching the catchword provided in the metadata for the next page.</note>

<term>state of nature</term><note>original: "Stande der Natur." A philosophical concept (popularized by thinkers like Hobbes, Locke, and Rousseau) describing the hypothetical condition of humanity before the development of organized society or government.</note>

<term>happiness</term><note>original: "Glückseligkeit." More than just a fleeting emotion, this refers to a state of flourishing or "felicity" that was a central goal of Enlightenment ethics.</note>

<vocab>Grand Master, Brothers, principles, reason, happiness/felicity, rules of prudence</vocab>

<summary>This page begins a formal Masonic address, arguing that every rational person must develop guiding principles and duties to achieve true happiness and navigate the world prudently.</summary>
<keywords>Grand Master, Enlightenment philosophy, state of nature, reason, ethics, principles of action, happiness, rules of prudence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the speaker's philosophical inquiry into the motivations behind human behavior, specifically following the previous page's assertion that every person acts according to certain principles to achieve an ultimate goal.</meta>

that one can be firmly convinced that, taken as a whole, this goal is uniform and consistent for everyone. For the primary wish and desire of every person is indisputably directed toward seeking and promoting **bliss** <note>original: "Glückseligkeit." In the 18th century, this term implied more than just temporary happiness; it referred to a state of profound well-being, fulfillment, and spiritual blessedness.</note> to the greatest possible extent. Consequently, everyone chooses a primary system for their <term>conduct of life</term><note>original: "Lebenswandel." This refers to one's moral behavior and the manner in which one leads their life.</note>.

The actual difference in this regard may well consist solely in the **means** used to achieve the ultimate goal of bliss, and in the **concept** that people actually form of it. I would have reason to believe that the number of variations regarding these opinions among sensually-minded people might be in almost equal proportion to the number of people themselves. One person seeks his highest bliss and contentment in the satisfaction of his passions, while another seeks only to satisfy his merely animalistic drives; others desire only an abundance of worldly goods, and in general, happiness for them is everything that flatters their <term>sensuality</term><note>original: "Sinnlichkeit." The faculty of the senses or the tendency to prioritize physical gratification over rational or spiritual concerns.</note> and elevates it.

Through this, however, **reason** and true **spiritual power** <note>original: "Geisteskraft." This encompasses intellectual strength, mental energy, and the power of the soul.</note> are suppressed; this alone prevents them from properly recognizing through these faculties that the extraordinary <term>transience</term><note>original: "Vergänglichkeit." The quality of being fleeting or temporary; a major theme in Enlightenment and Baroque thought regarding the vanity of worldly things.</note> of all these things could serve as the strongest proof of their inadequacy for achieving true human happiness. There are

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into human motivation and the pursuit of happiness. While the previous page discussed those driven by "sensuality" and animalistic drives, this page introduces the rational person who uses reason to determine their path.</meta>

...but there are also people who, through the use of their **reason** and their inner feeling, place their primary happiness in the knowledge of what is around them. They draw conclusions about themselves from this knowledge, reflecting on their origin, <term>vocation</term><note>original: "Bestimmung." In this context, it refers to one's destiny or the specific purpose for which a human being is created.</note>, and purpose, and from this, they establish a firm and unchangeable plan for their <term>conduct of life</term>.

Even if people in a <term>state of nature</term><note>A reference to the philosophical concept of humanity's condition before the existence of organized society and laws.</note> were to have reflected on themselves in this way, it still seems to me necessarily linked to the very idea of a rational being to have reflected on oneself, to have set a certain goal to achieve, and consequently to have determined a plan for its execution. This plan, moreover, may be appropriate to the actual nature of man, or it may merely be a consequence of his sensual perceptions.

But what is this plan—arisen through reflection on one's origin, ultimate goal, and vocation—other than what we are accustomed to call **Religion**? Religion must therefore be linked to the thought of a rational being, and a person without any religion would, in my opinion, have to be entirely deprived of reason and reflection.

Now it would be necessary to investigate: Which religion is most appropriate for true, pure reason? If a person, through the knowledge of nature, knows the origin of bodies...

...recognizes and sees how one being always brings forth another, and draws a conclusion from himself about the origin of his ancestors; thus he must finally arrive at the first cause of emergence or the Author of the existence of all beings. 

The Creator must therefore be recognized by him, and the whole creation proclaims to him His <term>omnipotence</term><note>original: "Allmacht." The quality of having unlimited power, a standard attribute of God in Enlightenment philosophy.</note> and wisdom, and with them all His other attributes as inevitably connected with them—in short, His all-encompassing perfection. These attributes of the Supreme Being clearly demonstrate to him the purpose of the entire creation, namely: to make all creatures happy. For everything around him proclaims the Lord's love, and everything instills in him: The Lord alone is love. 

Man, the noblest creature of the Most High, is therefore also destined for <term>happiness</term><note>original: "Glückseligkeit." While it translates as "happiness," in this philosophical context it refers to "bliss" or "beatitude"—a state of perfect well-being and spiritual fulfillment.</note>. The attainment of his happiness is the purpose that lies before him to achieve. 

Here, in brief, is the essence of all religion of a <term>purified reason</term><note>original: "gereinigten Vernunft." A term used during the Enlightenment to describe reason that has been cleared of superstition and purely emotional impulses.</note>: knowledge of the Creator and of one’s own happiness, and the endeavor to achieve the same. It remains, therefore, to consider in what his true happiness consists. Simply in the endeavor to know his Creator more and more, to reflect himself, as it were, in the great attributes of his Creator, and to choose them for imitation—

to choose, while conversely avoiding all actions that could hinder him in this endeavor. Here, the merely natural man has certain information regarding his origin and <term>destiny</term><note>original: "Bestimmung." This term carries the weight of one's "vocation" or "purpose" as ordained by a higher power.</note> here on earth; only the purpose he achieves through this—or, to put it briefly, his fate after death—remains uncertain to him. He sees conjectures and possibilities before him; however, he must necessarily expect a <term>revelation</term><note>original: "Offenbarung." This, dearest friends, has always been for me—aside from many other reasons—the most secure and irrefutable proof of a divine revelation. You have the goodness of the Most High to thank for this holy revelation, O mankind; and its teachings, combined with those truths already recognized through <term>reason</term><note>original: "Vernunft." This reflects the "Natural Religion" of the 18th century, which sought to harmonize faith with logical observation of the world.</note>, alone constitute a religion worthy of a godlike creature. Through it, and through the knowledge of nature as learned from our holy teachings, man alone can be perfectly happy here in this world.

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page (81) continues the argument from page 80, which explored the limits of human reason. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing the human need to "choose" actions that align with the Creator's attributes.</meta>

...choose, and conversely to avoid all actions that could be a hindrance to him in this endeavor. In this state, the merely <term>natural man</term><note>original: "natürliche Mensch." A person relying solely on their innate faculties and observation of the physical world, without the benefit of religious scripture.</note> has certain knowledge of his origin and his purpose here on earth; only the final goal he achieves through this—or to put it briefly, his fate after death—remains uncertain to him. He sees conjectures and possibilities before him; however, out of the infinite love and <term>grace of God</term><note>original: "Gnade Gottes."</note>, he must necessarily expect a revelation regarding this. The grace of the Most High guarantees that such a revelation cannot be lacking, for He can never leave His creatures in a state of sad and unsettling ignorance. Instead, He always provides aid where the tools granted to them are too weak to penetrate. 

This, most beloved, has always been to me—among many other reasons—the surest and most irrefutable proof of a divine revelation. You humans have the goodness of the Most High to thank for this holy revelation; its teachings, combined with the truths already recognized through reason, constitute the only religion worthy of a creature made in the image of God. And it is only this religion, most dear friends, that can coexist with <term>genuine masonry</term><note>original: "ächte Maurerei." This refers to the spiritual and ethical system of Freemasonry, which the author views as being in perfect harmony with enlightened religious faith.</note>. Through this religion, and through the knowledge of Nature as learned from our holy teachings, man alone can be perfectly happy here in this world.

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the necessity of combining esoteric knowledge with moral character. <page-num>84</page-num>

...little use, compared to the one who walks within **Masonry** <note>original: "Maurerei." In this context, it refers to the spiritual and moral path of the Order rather than just the organization itself.</note>, recognizes its teachings for the glorification of the great **Jehovah**, benefits himself and his fellow man thereby, and finally, when his course comes to an end, looks toward eternity with joyful eyes. How unhappy, by contrast, is he who, without any inner morality and integrity, attains even the greatest knowledge <note>The "greatest knowledge" likely refers to the higher degrees and secret teachings of the Order which were sought after by many in the 18th century.</note>, yet views it merely as an object of his curiosity or for certain worldly advantages, thereby neglecting the treasure to be gained from it for eternity. 

But a thousand times happy is he who, by despising what is temporal, transitory, and earthly, and through self-denial, has gathered for himself eternal goods. Then we shall laugh at you, you fools and mockers of religion, and go calmly with a serene spirit toward the way of life, defying death, hell, and the devil. 

For our **divine Master** <note>A reference to Jesus Christ, often referred to in Christianized Masonic rites of this period as the "Grand Master" of the celestial lodge.</note> is here, who through his all-bountiful merit removes the sins still clinging to us from weakness, makes us righteous, and supports us with the **power of his grace** <note>original: "Gnadenkraft." A theological term for the transformative power of God's favor acting upon the human soul.</note>, and who allows us to depart from this world to be with him forever in undisturbed joy. Then, with complete submission to the will of the Most High, we await that which the fool fears, which makes the wicked tremble, and which strikes the mocker to the ground.

# Especially esteemed Brother Installer. <term>Brother Installer: A specific Masonic title for the officer appointed to formally "install" or establish a new Lodge and its officers.</term>

## Worthy, dear brothers!

You finally experience today the glorious day on which you see the foundation being laid for your sanctuary <term>Sanctuary: A symbolic reference to the Masonic Lodge as a holy place of moral labor.</term>. What gratitude do we not owe our God for this! It is a new sign of His grace and goodness, in that He permits you, unanimously and united, to pursue the business of your pure and infallible bliss. I therefore have no doubt that you will at all times make it your highest concern to walk along the path of virtue and integrity, guided by the divine precepts of true wisdom. Your previously expressed good conduct and sincere intentions, my brothers <note>original: "Brüder"; the standard fraternal address for members of the Lodge.</note>, leave me with not the slightest doubt that you shall exert all your strength to carry out this precious undertaking; on the contrary, I am convinced — that you will make it your concern to improve your failings, to elevate your **spirit-man** <term>Geistmenschen: In 18th-century German thought, this refers to the higher, intellectual, or spiritual self as opposed to the physical or animalistic self.</term> above **sensuality**, to strive for the love of your fellow human beings—yes, even your enemies—and above all for the glorification of your great **Creator**, in order to contribute at all times new stones to the **temple of virtue** consecrated to God. To live uprightly and virtuously in one’s worldly station, to be faithful to one’s calling, and to live in accordance with the will of the Most High; to trustingly surrender all one’s fates to God, to remain faithful to Him and to the **teachings of the Order** <note>original: "Ordenslehren"; the specific moral and symbolic instructions provided to members of the Lodge.</note> until death, to promote the happiness of others to the best of one's ability and to find one’s own happiness within theirs, and especially never to forget the greatest duties: **obedience** and **discretion** <note>original: "Verschwiegenheit"; this refers to the Masonic obligation of silence regarding the secrets and internal business of the Lodge.</note>. Then you may rightfully uphold the chosen motto: **Virtue grows under vigilance and silence.** <note>original Latin: "Virtus sub vigilantia silentioque crescit."</note>

# Introduction.

Love and gentleness, those virtues which alone characterize the true nature of the religion of Jesus, are the very qualities that distinguish the genuine worshiper of Christ and present him as such before the eyes of the whole world. To wish to be a <term>Mason</term> <note>original: "Maurer"; a member of the Freemasons, a fraternal organization focused on moral and intellectual self-improvement.</note> or a true seeker of truth within practical Christianity, and yet not to bear these genuine marks upon oneself, nor to strive ever more to truly adopt them, would be to demand an effect without a cause. For from love alone flow genuine actions that harmonize with our inner sentiments. For a time, actions may dazzle the weak eyes of men through their outward veneer—men who must be satisfied with the apparent shell—while the all-seeing eye of the <term>Ruler of the World</term> <note>original: "Weltenregierer"; a common Enlightenment-era term for God as the rational governor of the universe.</note> unfolds hearts completely and beholds them in their nakedness. And yet even this lasts only for a while, after which that which was <note>The speaker uses the metaphor of "whitewashing" (übertüncht) to describe hypocrisy or superficial virtue.</note> whitewashed is nevertheless discovered, and the shameful exposure of ignoble intentions is recognized all the more clearly. — How contemptible, therefore, are all actions whose source is merely the desire of men

## Part One.

Love is a longing and desiring feel-
ing to become more closely acquainted,
and where possible united, with an object that
draws our entire attention to itself. The sen-
ses, acting as <term>conductors</term> <note>original Latin: "conductores"; the author uses this term to describe the senses as the channels or "guides" that carry information from the outside world into the mind.</note>, which bring external objects
to our mind and our feelings,
are what first cause some pleasure to the
human being whose intellectual powers are begin-
ning to develop; and this can already be called
love, albeit in the slightest de-
gree. However, the more the intellect and
the body develop, the more this
love increases. But when the intellect finally
reaches maturity and is so far developed
that it can draw conclusions from objects
located outside itself back to itself, and
when education furthermore strives to en-
lighten his intellect and to present God
to him as the author of all his current child-like joys,
then there arises an inner feeling of
gratitude—often silent, yet lively—toward
the author of these joys. As this is
continually increased and strengthened, there arises
from it a true, firm love for God in
riper years, which allows one to conclude <term>by induction</term> <note>original Latin: "per inductionem"; a method of reasoning where one draws a general conclusion (God is good) from specific instances (past experiences of joy).</note>
that, even if the ways and providences
of God often do not correspond to our wishes,
the God who otherwise always [strives] to gladden us and

Love for God is now the primary source of pure feelings, and thus also the sole <term>primal source</term> <note>original: "Urquelle"; the original or ultimate fountainhead.</note> of good actions, which become, as it were, a sacrifice offered to God in gratitude. # Part Two

The love of God reveals itself to us through its beneficial and blessed influence upon all creatures. What a motivation this is for us to follow that most perfect example as much as possible, and to do good within our own small <term>sphere of influence</term> <note>original: "Wirkungskreise"; literally the circle in which one acts or has an effect.</note>, insofar as God grants us the strength to do so.

But if this "good" is to be of a genuine nature, it must also correspond to the dispositions of the heart and be founded upon affectionate <term>feelings</term> <note>original: "Empfindungen"; refers to the refined moral sentiments central to 18th-century thought.</note> toward those creatures connected to us. In short: love for one’s <term>fellow human beings</term> <note>original: "Nebenmenschen"; those who live alongside us; neighbors in the broadest sense.</note> must be our first and most essential duty, following the example of divine love. 

To be able to fulfill this duty in its full scope, however, requires a precise examination of our own inner feelings and the senses of the heart, in order to discover whether they are in harmony with our actions. This investigation is the primary duty toward ourselves, from which all others first arise. Every duty is an <term>obligation</term> <note>original: "Obliegenheit"; a formal responsibility or task one is bound to perform.</note> that arises through the longing for the preservation of some "good," and the harmony...

First, therefore, the nature of the good we wish to attain must be examined, and only then the obligation which is to help us toward it. We have already determined that the thinking, practical <term>worshiper of Christ</term> <note>original: "Christus-Verehrer"; a person who honors or follows the teachings of Christ.</note> can desire and demand no higher good than <term>communion with God</term> <note>original: "Gemeinschaft mit Gott"; a state of spiritual fellowship or union with the Divine.</note>, the source of all good, who through love constantly seeks to draw human beings to himself, and whose beneficial influences impart to them powers, the use of which is, as it were, a sacrifice offered to him. For everything we have, do, and effect—is God's, and therefore belongs to him; and it is a duty to increase it for his <term>glorification</term> <note>original: "Verherrlichung"; the act of honoring or exalting God's name.</note>, as with an <term>entrusted talent</term> <note>original: "anvertrauten Pfunde"; a reference to the biblical Parable of the Talents (Luke 19), where a "pound" or "talent" represents gifts or responsibilities to be managed for a master.</note>. In short, all our duties must aim toward this: acquiring the great treasure of communion with God. We possess pure feelings implanted in us by the highest source of good; these belong entirely to God and must be directed solely toward Him. We have the capacity for the examination of ourselves and of all objects outside of ourselves; finally, we have powers that we can apply to the benefit of our <term>fellow creatures</term> <note>original: "Nebengeschöpfe"; all living things created alongside us.</note>. From this, three <term>primary duties</term> <note>original: "Hauptpflichten"; the fundamental moral requirements.</note> can be determined.

1) Duties toward God.
2) Duties toward ourselves, and
3) Duties toward our fellow human beings.

The primary duties toward God are undoubtedly: love, as gratitude for all the <term>benefits</term> <note>original: "Wohlthaten"; acts of kindness, favors, or blessings bestowed by God.</note> shown to us; the <term>fear of the Lord</term> <note>original: "Furcht des Herrn"; in this context, it refers to a profound awe and reverence that leads one to avoid anything displeasing to God, rather than a paralyzing terror.</note> arising from it, or an abhorrence for all vice displeasing to Him; the deepest reverence for the greatness of His majesty; submission to His most wise will, which, through His grace and goodness, seeks only to constantly promote our true <term>happiness</term> <note>original: "Glückseligkeit"; a state of supreme well-being, spiritual blessedness, or fulfillment.</note>; and which, through the paths and ordinances of His wisdom, always chooses the best means for the best ends; [and finally] trust in God, as a consequence of this sincere devotion.

And now finally, the duty so wholesome and bringing of happiness to us: that of prayer. But may I indeed call a permission granted for our happiness a "duty"? Might I not rather call it a delightful bliss and a joy-bringing call of grace, when a voice from heaven comes to us: <note>original: "Bittet, so wird euch gegeben"; a direct quotation of the promise found in the Gospels of Matthew (7:7) and Luke (11:9).</note> "Ask, and it will be given to you." To not receive a call of this kind with a grateful, moved heart would be to disdain it. Prayer and faith in the divine promises are inseparably linked. Let us therefore, most dear worshipers

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the duty of prayer as an expression of the "religion of Jesus" before transitioning to the second category of moral obligations: duties toward oneself.
