—= ☙ =—

...of the religion of Jesus: pray without ceasing. This means that our conduct should be the expression of the feelings of our hearts toward God, and our entire life should be a constant prayer. These are the primary [duties] toward the Most High, condensed into the greatest possible brevity due to a lack of sufficient time.

The duties toward ourselves are of a moral and physical nature; the latter concern merely the coarser part of our being, the body, and extend to self-preservation and care for one’s health. As long as we recognize that life and health are <term>talents</term> <note>original: "Pfunde"; literally "pounds," referencing the biblical Parable of the Talents/Minae (Matthew 25/Luke 19), where servants are expected to invest what their master gives them.</note> received from the wise hand of Providence, to be <term>put to use</term> <note>original: "wuchern"; literally to practice usury or increase through investment. In this moral context, it means to use one’s life and health productively for good ends.</note> according to their purpose, then the necessity of caring for them is sufficiently clear—so long as higher duties toward God and fellow human beings do not demand the opposite of us. To make an idol out of these [physical things], however, and to neglect one's higher obligations regarding moral duties toward oneself, would be to show more honor to the shadow than to the object itself. If I now wish to determine the duties toward myself in a moral sense, I must seek to gain an accurate knowledge of myself; I must therefore examine myself in order to achieve <term>self-knowledge</term>. With true impartiality, I must then [unfold] my heart to myself

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on moral duties toward oneself, specifically the "unfolding" of the heart mentioned at the end of page 107. It transitions from internal self-improvement to the duties owed to one's fellow man.</meta>

unfold, and it becomes my duty to banish everything that appears to me corrupted, or more subject to sin and vice than to virtue. This is not easy; rather, it is certainly subject to manifold difficulties and arduous labor. Therefore, given the current corruption of the human heart, it becomes the work not merely of days or years, but unfortunately of a whole lifetime. Thus, the cultivation of our selves and the constant struggle against the passions within us—which contend against the will of God—remains the business of our entire lives. 

Our weakness and impotence never allow us to engage in this dangerous struggle alone, as the temptations of our own <term>flesh</term> <note>In this theological context, "flesh" (German: *Fleisch*) refers to innate human desires or weaknesses as opposed to the spirit.</note>, as well as those of the world and the influences of spiritual forces existing outside of us, are so numerous that we would certainly succumb. Therefore, only the support of a higher, divine power can protect us from a fall; to surrender ourselves to this power is our highest duty.

The duties toward our fellow human beings are so closely connected with those we owe to God that Scripture itself proclaims: **"He who does not love his brother whom he sees, how can he love God whom he does not see?"** <note>A reference to 1 John 4:20.</note> 

Accordingly, love is our first and primary duty toward our neighbor. What a happiness for every family, for every society, when harmony and love link the individual members together; and how vanished...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the social duties of man, specifically picking up from the end of page 108 regarding the absence of love and harmony in a community.</meta>

all happiness [is lost] as soon as those are lacking; immediately sorrow and a tormenting life take its place. May I (—) be permitted to judge others by myself (for a moment). What happiness it is to have one or more friends who, bound to us by true love, help us bear the worries and indeed the pains of this life, and share all our joys. It would be a greedy thought to enjoy all joys for oneself alone; nothing increases their inner value more than the happiness of being able to share them with others. And you, oh human, who wander along with a sickly body, a weary heart, and burdened with sorrow, misery, and wretchedness—what a comfort for you, even when you lack everything else, to find a sympathetic friend or benefactor who takes an interest in you with a sincere heart, and with whom you find relief simply by being allowed to pour out your pains into his bosom. Sorrow already half-disappears when, through a frank and well-received confession, one sees but a single tear roll down the cheek of our friend. However, this can only be weakly described; it must be felt for oneself. From love for one’s neighbor flow all other duties toward them, such as humility, which never allows—despite all the abilities and advantages received as gifts of grace from wise <term>Providence</term> <note>Providence (German: *Vorsehung*) refers to the protective care of God or nature as a spiritual power.</note>—that one

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 109, completing the sentence regarding how humility prevents a person from exalting themselves over those with fewer natural gifts.</meta>

exalts himself over another who has been gifted with fewer <term>pounds</term> <note>original: "Pfunden". A biblical reference to the Parable of the Talents or Pounds (Luke 19:11-27), referring to the spiritual or intellectual gifts one is expected to use for good.</note> of this kind; instead, this glorious duty of virtue necessarily drives one to apply all these advantages solely and exclusively for the well-being and gentle instruction of his **fellow brethren**. Patience with the weaknesses and insults of our **fellow man** flows, in turn, from love. Only pride and self-conceit can be the reason for failing to follow this duty, since the consciousness of our own weakness and the endeavor to follow the example of our **divine Master** alone instill it in us. Compassion is the inevitable consequence of love. This drives the true **philanthropist** <note>original: "Menschenfreund", literally "friend of humanity".</note> to let the painful sensation—arisen within him through the suffering and sorrow of his brother—pass into active effort to relieve that brother's needs. True compassion is always active; if it is not, then it is merely <term>sentimentality</term> <note>original: "Empfindelei". In the 18th and 19th centuries, this term often criticized shallow or performative emotion that lacked practical action.</note>.

Here, **Dearest ones**, I have presented to you, though with the greatest brevity, a **general outline** of all the types of duties incumbent upon us. With more leisure and time, much more could be added and many deficiencies corrected; meanwhile, I hope to have presented at least the most essential points to you, and to have demonstrated the exact connection of **love for God** as the source of all good and the fulfillment of our duties.

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page follows the discussion of general moral virtues from the previous page, specifically concluding the transition from universal duties like compassion and humility to the specific obligations required of a member of the Masonic Order.</meta>

Allow me to add just three more principal **Masonic duties** <note>original: "Maurer-Pflichten". "Maurer" is the German word for Mason, referring here to the practitioners of Freemasonry.</note> specifically for us:

1) Loyalty to the **Order**.
2) Obedience to the **superiors**, and
3) **Discretion**. <note>original: "Verschwiegenheit". This refers to the Masonic virtue of keeping the secrets of the craft and the confidences of fellow members.</note>

The first obligates us to diligence and to the greatest effort on the paths of **divine wisdom** (for which we must necessarily hold the teachings of the Order—otherwise we are not **M.** <note>Abbreviation for "Maurer" (Mason).</note>) to constantly progress; for any standing still would be a setback, since a person either rises or falls in goodness; in this, there is no **middle ground**. If we are loyal to the teachings of the Order and diligent in the investigation of the truths presented to us therein, then obedience to the superiors follows naturally, as they then appear to us as **loving fathers**, ready to share with us the **higher revelations** <term>revelations</term> <note>original: "Aufschlüsse". In this context, it refers to the spiritual insights or "light" shared as a member advances through the degrees of the organization.</note> of which we have become capable. Trust in them then gives birth to obedience. **Gossip** betrays **frivolity**, and this can never be reconciled with the investigation of important subjects; only **seriousness**, a firm character, and the art of remaining loyal to one’s friends and benefactors through **discretion** make one capable of this.

<summary>This page defines the three core obligations of a Mason—loyalty, obedience, and discretion—positing that spiritual and moral growth must be a constant, active pursuit.</summary>
<keywords>Masonic duties, loyalty, obedience, discretion, divine wisdom, spiritual progress, Masonic Order</keywords>

## Most Worthy Friends!

What a boundless 
field of duties is incumbent upon us. — A 
glance directed toward ourselves, what im- 
potence, how little receptivity for 
goodness remains, alas — and yet another year is 
gone. — A crushing thought, to 
have lived through such a long time, to 
have come so much closer to the deci-

the time He entrusted to us as a

convinced that you all bear the true, heartfelt 
desire to improve and complete 
that which you still feel is deficient 
in yourselves, through total surren- 
der to the Will of the Most High, and patient 
expectation of the support of His higher power.

My constant and sincere wishes for your collective well-being, happiness, and preservation are too well known to you for it to be necessary to offer a new assurance of them here; only since certainly no one can be more sincerely devoted to you, or honor and love you more dearly than I, I wish only to ask for your continued confidence, brotherly love, and affection on this occasion, in the firm hope that you will kindly allow yourselves to be convinced of the endeavor with which I consider it my duty to make myself further worthy of them.

# Short Preface.
---

Many of my worthy and dear brothers expressed their satisfaction to me regarding the publication of my first collection of speeches, and cherished the desire for a second; in particular, however, a **Serene Brother** <term>Durchlauchtiger Bruder; a formal title used to address a member of the high nobility—such as a prince—within a fraternal order</term> commanded me to communicate my thoughts to him on various subjects in the form of short treatises. All of this moved me to the decision to make a second collection known to the public. My primary focus was to clearly demonstrate to the entire world that only through genuine...

<summary>In this preface, the author explains that the success of his first volume and a specific request from a high-ranking "Serene Brother" motivated him to publish this second collection of speeches and treatises.</summary>

<keywords>preface, collection of speeches, fraternal order, Serene Brother, treatises, 18th-century literature, enlightenment</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Short Preface." The text completes the sentence from the previous page, where the author was discussing how "genuine Christ-like spirit" is the only way to maintain the goals of the order. The catchword "ten" from the previous page concludes the German word "ächten" (genuine).</meta>

true Christ-like spirit <note>original: "Christus-Sinn"; referring to a mindset modeled after the character and teachings of Christ</note> could maintain the noble purpose of Freemasonry <term>Maurerey; a common 18th-century German term for Freemasonry</term>, and that the latter specifically aims to spread the religion of Jesus ever further. In these present times—when mockery, immorality, and sophistries <note>clever but fallacious arguments intended to deceive</note> of every kind attack religion from all sides, proceeding unpunished and unhindered under a tolerance that is indeed highly detrimental—the order's purpose is to provide a firm and unshakable bulwark against irreligion and the vice that necessarily springs from it. Furthermore, it seeks to save many a tender, unspoiled youth from ruin through instruction, guidance, and example. 

How greatly—I must here publicly confess it—do I lament so many a Lodge which, unmindful of its purpose, wanders in a perverse spirit and thereby goes astray. Such a Lodge must finally be ashamed for having waited so long in vain, without having attained any knowledge or advantages other than having been deceived, and in turn deceiving others. Oh! If my plea can have any influence over you,

Then, friends, you shall have nothing to fear; for benevolent Superiors <term>Wohlthätige Obere; high-ranking leaders of the Order who oversee the progression of members</term> will surely delight in revealing themselves to you, so that they may share the true light <note>a common 18th-century metaphor for spiritual wisdom and hidden knowledge</note> with you. — I once again most humbly request the kind indulgence of my esteemed readers regarding the speeches that follow here; they certainly do not boast of elegance or the art of rhetoric <note>original: "Redekunst"; the author is using a common historical "modesty trope," claiming his writing is simple to emphasize its sincerity</note>. ### Highly and Dearly Esteemed by All

->Brothers of the Order!<-

When I venture to speak before men so enlightened and so well-acquainted with the truths of the Order, it is partly because some of them have commanded me to do so, and I am bound to fulfill the <term>Masonic duty</term> <note>original: Maurer Pflicht</note> of obedience. It is also because your indulgence—combined with love and gentleness, those true hallmarks of a Mason—gives me courage. I am convinced that through your profound insight, you will complete what is lacking, correct what is flawed, and above all, regard the good intentions behind this work more than the elegance of its phrasing.

# Most Worthy, Dearest Friends!

Across the entire face of the earth, there is, as it were, a constant echo of the sincere longing and the loud desire of all people to become content, peaceful, and happy. All of nature and every creature <note>original: Creatur; referring to all living things in the created order</note> yearns for happiness, and the masterpiece of creation, destined to be its master—man—harbors this wish, so appropriate to his nature, most of all.

Nevertheless (and here I refer to the confession of all men who have hitherto been accepted everywhere as experts of the human heart), how little true, uniquely satisfying happiness is actually found among men? Should this be a consequence of a total lack of true happiness, or of the conduct of men in trying to partake of it?

We all know, Dearest Friends, a few people who, without living in any dizzying delusion, have proclaimed themselves happy, and whose inner peace—the only genuine hallmark—confirmed their statement. This sufficiently guarantees the real existence of a true bliss <term>Glückseligkeit; a state of deep, spiritual happiness or blessedness</term>, although the sufferings of injured sensuality <note>original: Sinnlichkeit; in this context, it refers to the physical senses and the worldly desires that can conflict with the spirit</note> always become detrimental to moral contentment through their influence; and thus, taken in the most abstract sense, a perfectly complete happiness is, for this reason, just as little to be found in hu-

<vocab>Happiness, Bliss/Blessedness, Creation, Creature, Confession, Sensuality, Contentment, Humanity</vocab>

<meta>The text ends mid-word with "Men-", which the catchword "schen" identifies as "Menschen" (humanity/mankind) continuing on the next page.</meta>

<summary>The author begins a philosophical reflection on the universal human quest for happiness, noting that while true bliss is rare and often disrupted by physical suffering, its existence is proven by the inner peace of a few enlightened individuals.</summary>
<keywords>happiness, human nature, creation, sensuality, moral contentment, Glückseligkeit, philosophy of the heart</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of happiness. The previous page ended with the syllable "hu-", which is completed here as "humans" (translated from the German "Menschen," where the first syllable "Men-" appeared on the previous page).</meta>

mans can take place. When one further considers the sphere of activity of our mental <note>original: geistigen; this encompasses both the spiritual and the intellectual faculties</note> abilities, which is currently so greatly restricted by the physical body <note>original: sinnlichen Körper; referring to the body as an organ of the senses, which in Enlightenment philosophy was often seen as a limiting "prison" for the soul</note>, whereby we are constantly cast into a labyrinth of doubts regarding the most important matters most necessary for our peace, we easily perceive the imperfection of our bliss <term>Glückseligkeit; a term for deep, spiritual happiness or "blessedness" that transcends mere worldly pleasure</term> through the connection in which it stands with our other imperfections. 

Thus, whenever I speak here of true happiness, I necessarily mean only that of which we are capable by virtue of our imperfection. That we can, however, become partakers of it is proven to us by experience, as has already been mentioned. To this end, let one behold a dying, true worshiper of Christ, who calmly on his deathbed offers to his beneficent Creator the purest sacrifice of thanks for all the blessings received in this life, and leaves this world with joy to become a partaker of higher benefits and bliss <note>The "peaceful death" was a major theme in 18th-century literature, serving as the ultimate evidence of a life lived in accordance with faith and reason</note>. 

Experience thus seems to have proven decisively that true happiness, appropriate to our nature, can indeed take place. On the other hand, however, it also proves daily how few people become partakers of this bliss, and how many wander about plagued by doubts, restless and unsteady. Since true happiness has now been proven to exist here below, and

<meta>The catchword "den-" at the bottom of the page indicates that the next page begins with a word starting with those letters (likely "dennoch" or a similar connective).</meta>

<summary>The author argues that while human happiness is limited by our physical bodies and inherent imperfections, the peaceful death of a devout believer serves as empirical proof that a "true bliss" is achievable on earth.</summary>
<keywords>bliss, imperfection, physical body, doubt, deathbed, Creator, experience, religious devotion</keywords>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's argument regarding human happiness. nevertheless, few people become partakers of it, it seems to me to be merely the fault of humans when they miss it. Either they do not know the actual great value of true bliss <term>Glückseligkeit; a state of supreme, divine happiness or "blessedness" that transcends worldly pleasure</term>, or they do not know the means to reach it. To further illuminate this subject, it seems necessary to me to undertake reflections on the true bliss and contentment of man.

For, although it is common in only a few Masonic <note>original: Maurischen; referring to Freemasonry</note> assemblies to lay words of Holy Revelation <note>The Bible or Holy Scripture</note> as the foundation for reflections befitting the truths of the Order <note>The Masonic Order</note>, I nevertheless cannot refrain from doing so. For I proclaim freely and publicly that I am not ashamed of the Gospel of Christ <note>original: "Evangelii von Christo"; a direct reference to Romans 1:16: "For I am not ashamed of the gospel of Christ..."</note>, though it may always be a folly to the mockers and an offense to the non-Christians <note>original: "Thorheit" and "Aergernis"; a reference to 1 Corinthians 1:23 regarding the preaching of Christ</note>. This is all the more so because I must sincerely confess that, until now, I have always drawn all comfort, all peace, and contentment from this divine source of wisdom; and I moreover have the good fortune to speak before listeners who, in the true sense of genuine Masonry, know no other path to the goal than that through Christ <note>original: "Christum"</note> our Lord and Master.

To these present brief reflections on the true bliss and contentment of man, the words of the Apostle shall therefore...

<meta>This page continues the theological distinction between different types of faith, following the previous page's concluding thought on the necessity of differentiating them from one another.</meta>

...to distinguish from one another, for the proper enlightenment of everyone. Faith <term>Glaube; in this context, a deeply held conviction or belief</term> in general seems to me to be an opinion adopted from inner conviction. The ground of this conviction determines the nature of faith. If it is our reason <term>Verstand; the human faculty of logic and analysis</term> that produces this faith through the investigation of reasons for or against the object of our attention, then worldly faith <term>Weltglaube; faith based on human observation and logic rather than revelation</term> arises.

But as soon as God alone, through His blessing-rich influences and through engagement with His Holy Word, brings this faith forth within us, it becomes a pure divine faith. As an inevitable consequence of this, the reason is enlightened—rendered capable of genuine investigation and cleansed of prejudices—while the heart (as the capacity for receptivity <term>Empfänglichkeits=Vermögen; the inherent ability to receive or be affected by spiritual truth</term> toward the good) is drawn ever closer to the Most High, and thus becomes increasingly ready for righteousness <term>Rechtschaffenheit; the state of being morally upright and just</term> and holy virtue.

Divine faith, therefore, is solely capable of making a person calm, satisfied, and happy.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between faith and human logic. It is by no means my intention here to detract anything from the intellect <term>Verstand; the human faculty of logic and understanding</term> and reason <term>Vernunft; the higher faculty of rational thought</term>, these so noble gifts of God, for which we owe Him our warmest and most sincere thanks; no, I prize them highly, and certainly higher than many others. However, I cannot help but perceive in them powers and abilities that could reach out very far and aim toward our perfection <term>Vervollkommnung; the process of moral and spiritual completion</term>, but which are so restricted by our sensuality <term>Sinnlichkeit; here referring to human nature's inclination toward the physical senses and worldly desires</term> that they are everywhere not only halted and hindered, but forced to stand quite still.

I did not wish, therefore, to suppress the intellectual powers of man, but rather to enlighten and expand them; and that is the meaning of the Scripture when it speaks of the "taking captive of reason under faith" <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 10:5: "...and bringing into captivity every thought to the obedience of Christ."</note>. One should recognize its [reason's] impotence and weakness in its current state, and through submission to the higher power of God—the influence of faith through the Divine Word—strive to achieve an expansion of the sphere of activity for the spiritual faculties.

In the words of the text, the Apostle proclaims that he has finally found this true bliss <term>Glückseligkeit; a state of supreme spiritual happiness</term>—which he had sought in vain with his reason in the sciences and in worldly wisdom <term>Weltweisheit; philosophy or secular knowledge</term>—in the faith worked within him through Christ, which has provided him with peace with a just Judge,

Wahre Weisheit <note>True Wisdom</note>, proves itself in the sincere effort to choose the best means for the highest ends. Every human being, however, possesses a certain innate pride which, born of self-love <note>German: *Eigenliebe*; in 18th-century moral philosophy, this refers to an egoistic self-regard that contrasts with "neighborly love."</note>, drives them to assign a certain value to all their actions, and to pay more heed to the consequences and the impression they make on others than to the intentions behind them. These intentions, however, are what solely determine the moral value of our actions, since neither the consequences nor the impression they make on others are within our power; instead, they often become the quite singular dispensations of divine providence <note>The original German uses *Vorsicht*, which in this context specifically refers to the theological concept of Divine Providence—the way God guides the world.</note>. Pride, then, is the primary object of our labor. Reason is far too weak to conquer it; for unfortunately, the more we are granted this glorious gift of the Most High, the more we fall into the temptation of exalting ourselves through it, and of fancying ourselves possessed of great strength. Divine faith, however, which continually leads us back to our own weakness and impotence by cleansing and enlightening our reason <term>Vernunft; the human faculty of logical thought, which Enlightenment thinkers prized but which the author argues is insufficient on its own</term> from prejudices, alone overcomes pride. I believe, therefore, that I can present the banishment of pride to you, Dearest Friends <note>A term of fraternal endearment typical of 18th-century secret societies or spiritual circles.</note>, as a primary fruit of faith; and if it were to grant us only this one advantage, oh, how healing it would be for us, and how much it would have to become the object of our most sincere striving! — The most enlightened concepts <term>Begriffe; mental representations or clearly defined ideas which the author believes must be properly ordered to reach truth</term>, and the proper ordering of them, through which

## Delivered

# on the occasion of the Visitation

### of the L[odge] Fr[iedrich] to the G[olden] Sc[epter]

## Dearest and Highly Esteemed Brothers of the Order!
<note>original: "Ordens-Brüder." In the context of 18th-century German Freemasonry, the "Order" refers to the fraternal organization as a whole, modeled after medieval knightly orders.</note>

Union into a society, in order to achieve a predetermined <term>ultimate purpose<gloss>original: "Endzweck." A philosophical term, often used in Enlightenment thought to describe the final goal or reason for a thing's existence.</gloss></term> through industriousness and collective labor, was the basis for the origin of <term>Masonic gatherings<gloss>original: "Maurischen Versammlungen." Literally "Masonic" or "Mason-like" meetings.</gloss></term>. The object of their activities led them toward their goal; and if it did not do so, then it would be mere folly to undertake anything without sufficient reason. — In the opposite case, however, the nature of the ultimate purpose to be achieved determined the moral value of the society itself, insofar as that society strove with more or less zeal to reach it. Therefore, in order to accurately determine the overall utility or dignity of a society, it is necessary to be precisely acquainted with its purpose. For every deeply thinking person, who is incapable of committing an act of any importance out of mere carelessness 

<summary>The speaker begins his address by arguing that the value of any society, including Freemasonry, is defined by its ultimate purpose and the collective labor used to achieve it. He asserts that a society's dignity is measured by the zeal with which it pursues its moral goals.</summary>

<keywords>Society, Masonic Gatherings, Ultimate Purpose, Labor, Moral Value, Utility, Enlightenment Philosophy, Brunswick Freemasonry</keywords>

## What the Nature of a Mason’s Work is, and in What it Consists?

The first words of our text show us the foundation of all our labors: namely, that we should strive so that everyone, even those outside our <term>Order<gloss>The specific Masonic fraternity or branch to which the speaker and audience belong.</gloss></term>, recognizes us as servants of Christ our Lord and Master. — Christ then, the Lord, who condescended to human nature in order to be more closely united with us, whose preaching and example were nothing but love toward the human race, and whose hand still blessed mortals on <term>Golgatha<gloss>The hill outside Jerusalem where Jesus was crucified; used here to emphasize mercy even during sacrifice.</gloss></term>, He who, as Creator and knower of their hearts, saw through the deception of His enemies, forgave them their raging fury against Him, and still prayed for them to His heavenly Father; Christ—love in life, in death, and now in His glory—is the one whom we pledged ourselves to serve upon entering the Order, and under whose banner of the cross we solemnly vowed to fight upon His holy word; He is the <term>cornerstone<gloss>original: "Eckstein." A primary stone in a structure; a common biblical and Masonic metaphor for a foundational principle or person.</gloss></term> upon which the entire building of our Order is erected, whom only foolish <term>builders<gloss>original: "Bauleute." A reference to people who attempt to build moral or social systems while rejecting spiritual foundations.</gloss></term> could reject, whose own construction then corresponded to their folly. — Christ is to be considered in a twofold way as our Lord and predecessor in the Order: first, as our loving <term>Reconciler<gloss>original: "Versöhner." A theological term for Christ’s role in bridging the gap between humanity and God.</gloss></term> with God, and second, as our constant model

# Dearest, most beloved brothers!

Dreadful is the sight of the licentiousness of morals caused in our times by the complete denial of all morality and religion—a state which a gentle tolerance covers and favors under the shadow of its wings. <note>The speaker is expressing a common late-18th-century anxiety: that "Enlightened tolerance" was being misused as an excuse for atheism or a lack of moral restraint.</note> True enlightened concepts, I thought, should only lead man to determine his genuine, pure happiness <note>German: *Glückseligkeit*. In this era, this referred not just to a fleeting emotion, but to "eudaimonia"—a state of flourishing or supreme blessedness achieved through virtue.</note> with the greatest precision, and to indicate the best means to achieve it.

Since the true happiness of a thinking, searching mind—which always directs its gaze and hope more toward the future than the present—cannot possibly aim at the minor sensual pleasures, whose short duration and lack of permanence fail to satisfy its expansive abilities and the vast field of its desires, true joys must necessarily relate to its higher powers. Thus, true happiness can only aim at the elevation of our mental faculties. <note>German: *Geistesfähigkeiten*. This refers to the intellectual and spiritual powers of the human soul.</note>

Now, experience proves that these faculties suffer and are worn down by nothing more than the inappropriate use of sensual pleasures. — And now, precisely these <note>The page ends with the catchword "diese" (these), leading into the next page's argument about the misuse of reason or pleasure.</note>

***

A key Enlightenment value, though here the author warns against its "shadow side."</note>, <term>Happiness</term> <note>German: *Glückseligkeit*. perience, or the grounds of his **reason** <note>German: *Vernunft*. In 18th-century thought, Reason was considered the primary tool for evaluating both the physical world and spiritual claims.</note>. Both lead him—if he wishes to proceed truly and in a certain philosophical order—to the source of all **perfection**, and the author of his existence. This knowledge can lead him to the presumption of a divine **revelation**, which determines for him his relationship with that highest source of all perfection, and shows him the means to keep himself happy and contented here below. 

If he finally receives a work which is described to him as such a revelation, he investigates with the grounds of his reason and with all sincerity whether this work bears the marks of divinity within itself; whether the means for promoting the **happiness** <note>German: *Glückseligkeit*. This term implies more than just "feeling good"; it refers to a state of deep, lasting blessedness or spiritual well-being appropriate to human nature.</note> of man are appropriate to his nature, and whether they correspond to the attributes of the **most perfect Being** <note>A common Enlightenment term for God, framing the divine as the logical pinnacle of all positive qualities.</note>; after this exact investigation—which is only meant to serve him in finding truth and comfort for himself—he makes his determination according to the most prevailing reasons he may have for or against the opinions contained in that same work, and thus he corresponds to the character of thoroughness and firmness. 

The mocker, on the other hand, laughs at everything, but proves neither the falsehood he has found in the opinions of others, nor the truth of what he has recognized as true. ...being to honor that **Being**, both through certain external marks of respect and through the fulfillment of those duties which are appropriate to our nature and the divine nature, and through which we enter into a certain communion with God, according to the teachings of the Book which we Christians revere as a true divine **revelation**. 

I do not intend here to speak against the **atheists**, whose folly rational pagans <note>German: *Heiden*. The author refers to classical philosophers (like Socrates or Cicero) who argued for the existence of a creator based on logic alone, without the benefit of the Bible.</note> have already sufficiently proven through the mere light of **reason**; rather, I speak only against those who indeed believe in and accept a **Supreme Being** but mock all other teachings of the Christian religion. I do not even speak to those who doubt, for it is not mine to judge; since only their own conscience will judge them, they may examine themselves to see if their doubts are sincere, or whether they have neglected to seek the truth using the faculties granted to them by **Providence** <note>German: *Vorsehung*. A central Enlightenment concept referring to God’s guardian care and the purposeful governance of the universe.</note>. 

I speak to those mockers of religion whose **immorality** is the cause of their conduct. If such a person found something in any man's religion—based on the concepts I have just established—which did not correspond to it, and was either unsuited to the nature of man or unbecoming of the divine essence, I think that if he wished to act wisely, he would point out these improprieties along with his reasons; but this part...

--- Page 219 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument against religious skeptics. It begins mid-word, completing the term "besonders" (particularly/especially) from the previous page. The author shifts from a general defense of belief to a specific defense of the "Religion of Jesus" (Christianity), framing it as a system designed for human benefit and grounded in the concept of free will.</meta>

...particularly with the intent of providing mankind with an essential advantage thereby, and out of reverence toward **God** himself. But is it not **folly** to merely mock another and to keep the reasons and, so to speak, the means of salvation for oneself? It seems to me almost as if one saw a man drowning, laughed at him, but did not come to his aid to save him. But when, on the contrary, nothing unseemly is found in a religion—especially regarding the veneration of the **Creator** and those **duties** one is obligated to perform for Him in order to promote one’s own **happiness** <note>German: *Glückseligkeit*. In 18th-century philosophy, this term refers to a state of profound well-being or "blessedness" that is the ultimate goal of human life.</note>—then mockery is not merely folly, but a **crime** <note>German: *Frevel*. This suggests a sacrilegious transgression or a moral outrage against something holy, rather than just a simple mistake.</note>. Let us examine this more closely.

The **religion of Jesus** is the greatest **offense** <note>German: *Aergernis*. In a theological context, this refers to a "stumbling block" (the Biblical *skandalon*), or something that causes spiritual scandal or resistance.</note> to the mocker. Suppose a doctrine were to proclaim: **God** desired to create a being, equipped with spiritual and physical faculties, and intended it to remain in constant communion with Him, so as to support it with the powers of perfection and to make it happy through an ever-increasing **knowledge** of the divine essence itself; [suppose] this being were created, and that man were furthermore given a **free will**, through which he was free to either uphold or violate the duties he owes, as a creature, to the **Author** of his **existence**.

<summary>The author argues that mocking religious belief is a moral failing, comparing the mocker to someone who refuses to save a drowning man. He presents the Christian faith as a logical system intended to lead humans toward perfection and happiness through the use of their free will.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical defense of Christian doctrine. It picks up from the previous page's discussion of a created being (humanity) intended for communion with God. The author is framing the "Fall of Man" and the "Redemption through Christ" not just as dogmas, but as a practical system that a rational person should test through experience rather than dismiss with mockery.</meta>

This being should rightly have remained obedient to the same <note>Referring to the "Source of all perfection" mentioned on the previous page.</note> and surrendered itself to its guidance in order to ascend ever higher toward the **source of all perfection**; yet it became disobedient by virtue of its **free will** <term>free will (German: *freyer Wille*): the capacity of a rational being to choose their own actions, a central theme in Enlightenment debates regarding moral responsibility</term>, and it fell. **God**, out of love, sent the emanation <note>German: *Ausfluß*. Literally an "outflow" or "effluence." This term was often used in mystical and philosophical texts to describe how the divine essence flows into the created world.</note> of His **perfection** in human form to reunite this fallen human being with Himself once more, and to show him the path to his own improvement and completion. Suppose now that all of this, and the means of which it is proclaimed that one need only test them to realize that this doctrine is from **God**, were found recorded in the book which is presented as the **divine revelation** <term>revelation (German: *Offenbarung*): the communication of sacred truths that cannot be known through reason alone</term>. —

The rational, thorough man would then judge the book according to what it provides itself, and would not first attempt to prove its inaccuracy to the whole world before he had at least attempted that which the book teaches—especially since it in no way proposes anything that could be contrary to human nature or entirely harmful to it.

Suppose a book were given to a physician containing a **medicine** <note>German: *Arzeney*. An archaic spelling of the modern German *Arznei*, meaning a remedy or pharmaceutical cure.</note> that was by no means lethal or harmful, but of which it was proclaimed that it had produced the most magnificent **effects** in the cases cited, and all of this were proven and confirmed by the best experiences, so that this remedy became publicly known

---

To you I turn, my worthy newly-received brother! Hail to you, that you have today dared the solemn step to tread the blissful path of Masonry <term>Masonry: Freemasonry, a fraternal organization that uses the tools of stoneworkers as symbols for moral and spiritual growth.</term>, where the noble skill of good actions pleasing to God, holy virtue, combined with a higher knowledge of the divine Being and his works, shall be the goal of your labor and your diligence. The work is great, dearest friend, and self-knowledge <note>original: *Selbsterkenntniß*; a central tenet of Enlightenment and Masonic thought, involving the rigorous examination of one's own character.</note>, mastery of sensuality <term>sensuality: original *Sinnlichkeit*; the state of being governed by physical senses and material desires rather than spiritual or rational principles.</term>, and submission to the guidance of a God who created us to make us happy, are its most essential parts. To wish to achieve the purpose of Masonry without these would be to demand an effect without submitting to the cause that gives birth to it. 

But there are also many difficulties that will encounter you on your untrodden path. ## Venerable, Highly Esteemed
# Order-Brothers! <note>original: "Ordens-Brüder." Members of a fraternal organization or secret society, in this case, the Freemasons.</note>

When a person recognizes a purpose as good and beneficial, it becomes their duty to strive for it with all their strength. Should they have been so fortunate, through their labor and endeavor, to have partially achieved this noble purpose, then this must drive them even further; through the enjoyment of the advantages arising from it, they must most eagerly seize the means to fulfill their intentions. From this follows a duty for us as well, dearest friends: once we have proper knowledge of our Masonic <term>Masonic: *Maurerisch*; relating to the principles and practices of the Freemasons, a fraternal organization centered on moral improvement and charity.</term> purpose, we must exert all our strength to achieve it. And should we have already partially attained it through the expansion of our knowledge and the enlightenment <term>Enlightenment: *Aufklärung*; a core 18th-century philosophical movement focused on reason, education, and the intellectual "clearing up" of concepts to improve the human condition.</term> of our concepts, then our zeal must be strengthened and fired up by this reward received for our labor. Then the actual moment is at hand where zeal must prove itself most active, as the Apprentice <term>Apprentice: *Lehrling*; the first and most foundational level of membership in Freemasonry, representing a seeker beginning their journey in the Craft.</term>, already more from one of the

### 3. That all untimely, incorrect zeal causes us to miss our goal.

Lukewarmness <note>Original: *Lauigkeit*; a state of spiritual or moral indifference often cautioned against in religious and fraternal texts.</note> in any good endeavor can only be produced by an incorrect knowledge of its purpose, or by the insight into the inadequacy of that purpose to achieve the set goal; a natural inclination toward laziness and an aversion to all activity can also contribute to this. From this it is clear that lukewarmness always causes one to fail in the intended final goal, including in <term>Masonry: *Maarerey*; an archaic spelling of *Maurerey*, the spiritual and moral system of the Freemasons.</term>. Not knowing its purpose, considering the means to it as inadequate, and having an aversion to activity are the causes of lukewarmness, which hinders all progress on this sublime career path toward true happiness <note>Original: *Glückseligkeit*; in the 18th-century context, this refers to a state of spiritual bliss and moral fulfillment rather than simple pleasure.</note>. To prevent this, let us illuminate the present matter more closely. The purpose of Masonry—the purpose of a thinking being united with others for the promotion of his true happiness—can only be the ennobling <note>Original: *Veredelung*; the process of refining human character from a "rough" state to a "polished," moral state.</note> of his corrupted, degraded nature, and the knowledge of the Supreme Being <note>Original: *höchsten Wesens*; a term for God commonly used by Enlightenment philosophers and Masons to emphasize a universal creator.</note>, through the combined lights of nature and revelation. To know this noble, sublime purpose and yet become lukewarm in one's zeal to achieve it seems impossible. However, no one knows it fully except he who perceives his own unworthiness and the sad decay of his nature, and self-knowledge

<summary>This page analyzes the causes of "lukewarmness" or indifference within the Order, identifying them as a lack of understanding of Masonic goals and a natural tendency toward laziness. It defines the ultimate purpose of Masonry as the moral ennoblement of the individual and the pursuit of divine knowledge through nature and revelation.</summary>

Knowledge alone awakens in us a true need for our perfection, and from this arises true zeal, which grows with the endeavor to ennoble oneself. The only effective means to the sublime purpose of <term>Masonry: *Maurerey*; the spiritual and philosophical system of Freemasonry, focused on moral building.</term>—the study of nature and revelation, the striving for genuine Christ-like virtue, submission to the guidance of the wisest Being <note>Original: *weisesten Wesens*; an Enlightenment-era term for God, emphasizing divine wisdom and reason.</note>, and trust in the same combined with prayer—to consider these glorious means as insufficient for achieving the sublime Masonic purpose testifies neither to an enlightenment of the intellect nor to good intentions of the heart.

When a certain natural laziness is added to this, lukewarmness <note>Original: *Lauigkeit*; a state of spiritual apathy or moral indifference, considered a significant failing in both religious and Masonic contexts.</note> is the necessary consequence, which displaces all zeal and makes the achievement of the purpose of Masonry impossible. Let us therefore avoid all lukewarmness, which can only be born of unenlightened concepts, a wicked heart, or laziness. You will soon see the harmfulness of this for the Order <note>Original: *Orden*; the specific branch or organization of Freemasonry to which the writer belongs.</note> when you consider what those who live outside the Order—and can only judge the tree by its fruits—must think of it when they see its members lukewarm and cold toward it; indeed, they can often conclude from their speech about the Order that they themselves attach no prestige to it. —

I speak before Masons who know their sublime purpose and revere the means to it; thus, lukewarmness will never take hold of you— <note>The text ends here mid-sentence, leading into the next page of the manuscript.</note>

...may dedicate himself to knowledge. <note>The text begins mid-sentence, concluding a thought about the dedication of the soul or intellect.</note> --- After attaining certain insights intended to strengthen our zeal for the **Order** <term>Order: *Orden*; the Masonic organization, viewed as a structured community with a shared moral purpose.</term> and to make us more skillful in the **work** <term>work: *Arbeit*; the Masonic process of self-improvement and ritual labor.</term> of achieving its purpose, lukewarmness is thus highly dangerous; I hope to have proven this adequately.    In almost all human knowledge, a certain standstill is a step backward; so it is also in **Masonry** <term>Masonry: *Maurerey*; the teachings and practices of the Freemasons.</term>. Pride magnifies the knowledge already received and persuades the weak person to deem the vast field of knowledge he still lacks as hardly worth a glance. Through this, the true moment of **effectiveness** <term>effectiveness: *Würksamkeit*; the active application of one’s abilities and Masonic principles in the real world.</term> is neglected. In such a moment, besides the abilities and strengths normally heightened and expanded by knowledge, this knowledge itself—which conveys experience and **discernment** <term>discernment: *Scharfinn*; intellectual sharpness or the ability to perceive subtle truths.</term>—could be unified. But as soon as pride lets its overwhelming power be felt, a standstill in the work arises, which becomes all the more harmful since the powers—which only attain proficiency through practice—are thereby completely hindered. The knowledge already received then becomes merely a burden, like a **dead pound** <note>original: *todtes Pfund*; an allusion to the biblical Parable of the Talents (Luke 19), where a "pound" or talent of money is buried in the ground rather than being put to use or "usury."</note> that cannot be put to interest. --- But precisely where the dangerous...

<meta>This page continues the speaker's analysis of pride as the greatest enemy of Masonic progress. It follows the discussion from the previous page regarding how a "standstill" in moral work leads to regression, specifically noting that pride is the force that halts this momentum.</meta>

...pride can most easily find a place, precisely where it can displace all **zeal** <term>zeal: *Eifer*; in a Masonic context, this refers to the earnest, active dedication to the Order's moral and philosophical goals.</term>, that is where our zeal must be most active. Pride suppresses all **self-knowledge** <term>self-knowledge: *Selbstkenntniß*; the practice of honest self-examination, which Masons consider the foundation of all spiritual growth.</term> and the **self-improvement** <term>self-improvement: *Selbstbearbeitung*; literally "self-working"; the metaphorical process of a Mason acting as a craftsman to "smooth" the rough stone of his own character.</term> based upon it—the effort to cleanse our inner selves of everything that makes us unreceptive to the good. The **thirst for truth** <term>thirst for truth: *Forschbegierde*; an inquisitive and longing spirit for discovery.</term>, that hallmark of the Divine which alone is capable of producing true zeal in **Masonry** <term>Masonry: *Maurerey*; the practices and moral system of the Freemasons.</term>, is likewise utterly destroyed by pride.

Zeal for a cause recognized as good, of any kind, can only spring from a good heart. Yet it is sad to have to confess that a passion which animates the majority of people cannot coexist with a good heart. Pride displaces **humility** and **love**, those essential qualities of the good heart. Only through them can true **virtue** be attained, for without love, every action is only seemingly good; love alone is the genuine source of **charity** <term>charity: *Wohlthätigkeit*; the active practice of benevolence and doing good for the welfare of others.</term>, of the gentle instruction of others, of humility, and of the low estimation of oneself...

are incapable of coexisting with **pride** <term>pride: *Stolz*; viewed here as the antithesis of the "rough stone" being smoothed by Masonic labor.</term>, 
but rather, pride is entirely contrary to them. 
Hence the words of the **Apostle** <note>original: "wem da dünket er stehe, der sehe wohl zu, daß er nicht falle." - A reference to 1 Corinthians 10:12: "Wherefore let him that thinketh he standeth take heed lest he fall."</note>: 
"let him who thinks he stands take heed lest he fall." 
He who has been so fortunate as to acquire **knowledge** <term>knowledge: *Kenntnisse*; specifically the moral and philosophical insights gained through Masonic degrees.</term> 
should be strengthened in his **zeal** <term>zeal: *Eifer*; the active, industrious dedication to the Order's principles.</term>, 
and apply it, along with his other powers and abilities, 
toward the achievement of his sublime purpose; 
but may pride never delude him so that he falls 
and misses the goal of his striving. — 
Let us therefore, most beloved <note>The speaker is addressing his "Dearest Brethren" (*Theureste*), a standard Masonic greeting.</note>, 
strive to acquire knowledge, so that we, 
driven by zeal, may then be intent on using it 
with strained efforts. Our zeal, however, 
should consist in avoiding everything that hinders 
the attainment of the intended goal, and thus 
in striving to learn precisely all those 
**passions** <term>passions: *Leidenschaften*; in 18th-century thought, these are the irrational impulses—like anger, lust, or envy—that a person must learn to control through reason.</term> 
that are most detrimental to our **virtue** <term>virtue: *Tugend*; the moral excellence that Masons seek to cultivate.</term>. 
This requires a precise and attentive **observation** <term>observation: *Beobachtung*; specifically "self-observation," the practice of monitoring one's own thoughts and motives.</term> 
of oneself, a complete unfolding of the 
most hidden corners of our hearts, and a 
distancing from all those opportunities 
that act upon its passions. 
Constant diligence and effort to [reach] this **ultimate purpose** <term>ultimate purpose: *Endzweck*; the final goal of moral perfection and harmony.</term>

<summary>The speaker argues that pride is incompatible with the gratitude owed to the Creator and warns that even those with great knowledge must guard against falling. He calls for rigorous self-observation and the mastery of one's passions as the only way to reach the ultimate goal of Masonic virtue.</summary>

attain, combined with the effort to gather the knowledge that grants us true contentment and serenity of spirit through the realization of our duties and of the **Supreme Being** <term>Supreme Being: *höchstes Wesen*; a common Enlightenment-era term for God used in Masonic and Deistic contexts.</term>, as well as His works. <meta>This page continues the moral and philosophical instructions for the Order, shifting from the warning against confusing literature to a practical guide on how a member should conduct their life. It emphasizes active virtue and social engagement over the isolation of ascetic monasticism.</meta>

Our main occupation should always be the diligence to strive, according to our best abilities, to draw closer to our **perfection** <note>perfection: *Vervollkommnung*; a central goal in 18th-century thought, referring to the constant moral and intellectual improvement of the individual.</note> in the fulfillment of our **duties** <note>duties: *Obliegenheiten*; the specific moral and social obligations one has toward God, society, and oneself.</note>. 

On the other hand, to renounce all human society, to **mortify** <note>mortify: *casteyen* (modern: *kasteien*); refers to the practice of self-denial or physical punishment of the body for religious discipline.</note> one's body, and to hope to please God through tiring prayer and singing—which is never pleasing to Him, and of which the Christian and the heart are not even mindful under the exhausting burden of the body—is to practice an irrational **service of God** <note>service of God: *Gottesdienst*; while often meaning a church service, here it refers to the broader way one honors the Divine through actions.</note> that is certainly not worthy of a human being. Such zeal would be foolish and certainly not appropriate for the attainment of our noble purpose. 

Let us revere the majestic God through the fulfillment and strictest observation of our duties, and through the true prayer of our hearts. Let us not, however, flee from people and their society, but rather be joyful with the joyful and weep with those who weep <note>A reference to the biblical exhortation in Romans 12:15, emphasizing empathy and social presence.</note>; conversely, through the constant example of the **fear of God** <note>fear of God: *Gottesfurcht*; meaning a respectful awe and reverence rather than terror.</note>, love for our **fellow men**, **chastity**, **humility**, **temperance**, and **charity**, let us be a model for our companions on this path of life to follow. 

Then we shall demonstrate a genuine and true zeal that will surely never let us miss our goal; but let everything be far from us that conflicts with **reason** <note>reason: *Vernunft*; the author views human logic as a "noble gift of God" which, when purified by grace, is the primary tool for moral living.</note>, this noble gift of God, and which is capable only of producing **fanaticism** <note>fanaticism: *Schwärmerey*; a derogatory term in the Enlightenment for irrational religious delusion or undisciplined emotionalism.</note> and folly.

So much for the harmfulness of an incorrect **zeal** <note>zeal: *Eifer*; in this context, it refers to an intense, sometimes over-eager dedication to the Order's principles or growth.</note> within the Order in regard to ourselves; we now consider to what extent it can become harmful to others. — Only sufficient reasons provide a sensible man with proof to confirm a truth. This is because no rational and thinking man can have a favorable opinion of a society whose members have adopted certain **principles** <note>principles: *Grundsäze* (modern: *Grundsätze*); the foundational rules or dogmas of the society.</note> without being able to prove them properly and convincingly. 

In such a case—since it can very often happen that one is perfectly convinced of a truth oneself without possessing the gift of presenting it clearly to another—one must first learn to recognize one's own weakness. One should strive to thoroughly acquire proper knowledge before being intent on communicating it to another. Furthermore, however, if we do not succeed in convincing someone of an accepted truth through our reasons, and we were to harbor hatred toward them because of it, this would no longer be merely misunderstood zeal. May pride never enter our minds to turn us from the path of virtue, but rather: **let him who thinks he stands take heed lest he fall.** <note>original: "dem da dünkt er stehe, der sehe wohl zu, daß er nicht falle"; a direct reference to the biblical warning in 1 Corinthians 10:12.</note> Amen.

->Saturday, April 10th, 1784.<-
During <term>Passion Week</term> <note>original: *Marterwoche*; the week before Easter, commemorating the suffering of Jesus.</note>, all the brothers residing in O. in S. gathered together without distinction of <term>systems</term> <note>In the 18th century, German Freemasonry was divided into various "systems" or rites, such as the Strict Observance and the Swedish Rite, which often had different organizational structures and rituals.</note> (being far too convinced that only a single purpose lies before the true <term>Mason</term>, and therefore they wished to see all differences joyfully banished). They met to hold a <term>Masonic assembly</term> hallowed in God for their mutual edification and encouragement toward the good, and for the honor of the Divine <term>Redeemer</term>. — Since there was no established <term>Lodge</term> in that specific place, the eldest brother took charge of the work and, at the very beginning, addressed the brothers in the following manner:

...a tool designed to communicate his thoughts and concepts to others through actions and words; a constant desire for pleasure and tranquility—all these advantages found within man, which, taken together with the noble gift of **Reason** <note>original: *Vernunft*; in Enlightenment and Masonic thought, this is the faculty that allows man to understand divine laws and govern his passions.</note> that defines their limits, elevate him so far above all creatures surrounding him. These provide him the surest proof that the highest source of all **benevolence** <note>original: *Wohlthätigkeit*; referring to the active goodness and charity of the Creator.</note> could only have given him existence for his own happiness. 

A desire to achieve this purpose is, as it were, the mainspring that animates all people and gives efficacy to our mental powers, so that through the **enlightenment** <note>original: *Aufklärung*; here used in the sense of clarifying or refining one's understanding to better perceive truth.</note> of concepts, we may choose the most suitable means for attaining this sublime goal. Consequently, man necessarily recognized that union with the source of all wisdom is alone capable of granting genuine advantages that satisfy both the intellect and the heart, and of establishing his true **bliss** <note>original: *Glückseligkeit*; a central 18th-century term for a state of lasting moral and spiritual well-being.</note>. 

Reason pointed him toward a stainless way of life and holy **virtue** as the means to effect this union with the purest source of all truth. Even now, when man has fallen so low from his original dignity through the **Fall of Man** <note>original: *Sündenfall*; the theological concept of humanity's transition from a state of innocent obedience to God to a state of guilty disobedience.</note>—even then, sound and purified Reason can suggest no other means. Only, the more enlightened the intellect becomes, and the more it is [acquainted] with the actual present nature of man...

<meta>This page continues the sermon from page 91 and 92, which introduced Matthew 11:28-29 as the central text. In the words of the text, the entire divine and great character of our Savior <term>Heiland: Savior/Healer</term> is, as it were, portrayed. With love and gentleness, he invites the person burdened by the weight of his sins to be refreshed by him—that is, to impart to him comfort and relief from his torturing anxiety of conscience <term>Gewissensangst: spiritual distress or the "pangs of conscience" regarding one's sins</term>. He commends his yoke <term>Joch: in a biblical context, this refers to a set of teachings or a way of life</term> to him, to take it upon himself, to submit to his precepts and the duties he prescribed for his happiness, and to learn from him that by which he can achieve his purpose. And this because he, the Lord of worlds and all knowledge, is nevertheless gentle and humble, and does not wish to punish or judge sinners, but rather to improve them and, in all humility, to show what can present them as free from sin and righteous before God, so that they might find rest for their souls, no longer anxious or tormented by constant reproaches, but rather convinced that "the souls of the righteous are in God's hand, and no torment touches them." <note>This is a quotation from the Book of Wisdom 3:1, often used in Christian liturgy to comfort the grieving or the repentant.</note>

Blessed are we <note>original: *Heil uns*; an exclamation of spiritual good fortune</note>, dearest friends! That we have such a model to follow in his footsteps; this is the first blessed fruit that grows for us from the sharing of his divine teachings, in that—

Although he was the Highest, he nevertheless chose to appear as the least among all, and only through superior blessings <term>Wohlthaten: good deeds or acts of charity</term> of every kind did he let his great superiority over the rest of the human race be felt. 

To combine a teaching with such deeds—a teaching that portrays only the blessings of God toward mankind and calls them to obedience toward him and to trust in the one who expressed strength and love through his example—is certainly the most healing event for a mortal who sincerely wishes to promote his own happiness <term>Glückseligkeit: a state of being blessed or true spiritual happiness</term>. <meta>This page continues the sermon's argument that Christ’s life serves as the ultimate moral blueprint. closest imitation <term>Nachfolge: The act of following or discipleship</term>. Therefore, dearest friends, let it be our primary focus in all occurrences of this life to always make a comparison with the conduct of Jesus <term>Wandel Jesu: The "walk" or way of life led by Jesus</term>; in all temptations to evil, in all ill-treatment by others against us, to notice what Christ did in such and far more important cases, and thus to undertake no action of any kind without making this wholesome and fruitful comparison. — Consider from the writings of antiquity <term>Alterthum: Referring here to the classical philosophy of Greece and Rome</term>, and unfortunately even of more recent times, how, without knowing the example of Christ, no perfect and sufficient system of morals <term>Sittenlehre: Ethics or moral philosophy</term> was ever established; how there is constant complaint that right and wrong in many cases cannot be determined by compelling reasons. We exchange conjectures <term>Muthmassungen: Assumptions or educated guesses</term> for certainty <term>Gewißheit: Spiritual or moral assurance</term>. — Christ humbled himself to become human <note>A reference to the theological concept of Kenosis, where Christ "emptied himself" to take on human form (Philippians 2:7).</note> in order to show man precisely that he demanded nothing from him that could be contrary to his nature or raised above it; no, in that very nature he fulfilled the entire sum of his teachings. <meta>This page continues the argument that while Christ’s nature is divine, he provides the necessary spiritual power to bridge the gap between human corruption and moral perfection. ...ted nature <note>The text continues from the previous page: "And although [Christ’s nature] could hardly be compared to the corrupted nature of man..."</note> could hardly be compared, the Redeemer wills for this very reason that man should seek fellowship with Him, in order to be cleansed from his corrupted nature and refined once again into his true original nature, and then, in undisturbed peace, attain the capability to do good—or in short, only then to be able to achieve virtue <term>Tugend: In this context, moral excellence that is practiced until it becomes a natural habit.</term>. 

Through His power, therefore, the divine Redeemer intends to compensate for what we lack, so that we may then follow His example and His teachings all the more securely; for without this power, such following cannot happen and would remain merely a pious intention <note>Literally "frommer Vorsatz," referring to a well-meaning resolution that lacks the strength to be realized.</note>. 

An example may serve here as an illustration. A sick and infirm body requires medicine, and the strictest diet <note>In the 18th and 19th centuries, "diet" (Diät) referred to a comprehensive regulated lifestyle or medical regimen, not just food.</note> can only ensure the permanence of the health he has recovered. Until the illness itself is lifted through the use of medicine, the diet can be of no use to him; it remains a good intention without the proper result. 

— The same applies here: without having entered into fellowship with Christ <note>original: "Christo" (Latin dative case common in German theological writing).</note>, all intention and diligence to do good is entirely without success, for the essence is missing—namely, the restoration and refinement of our corrupted nature through fellowship with Him, the Merciful One and Savior of our souls. Only then can we attain the capability to follow His precious example as a true disciple of the cross <term>Kreuzesjünger: A follower of Christ who identifies with His suffering and self-sacrifice.</term>. Hence the words of Jesus: "Take your cross upon you and fol-

<summary>This page explains that human virtue is only possible through a spiritual "healing" by Christ, using a metaphor of medicine being necessary before a healthy lifestyle can take effect. It emphasizes that following Christ's example requires divine power rather than mere human willpower.</summary>

"Follow me. <note>The text likely begins with the end of the phrase "Folget mir nach" (Follow me).</note> Together with your sins and vices, surrender yourselves to me, so that I may cleanse you from everything that prevents you from following my example." Therefore, in order to properly utilize the advantages arising for us from the religion of Jesus, let us always keep the example of its founder as our model; let us cast ourselves confidently into the loving arms of the merciful Redeemer, constantly imploring him for his assistance, so that he may cleanse us from every inclination toward vice and sensuality <term>Sinnlichkeit: In the 18th and 19th centuries, this term referred to the "lower" physical nature or worldly impulses that were thought to distract the soul from spiritual and moral growth.</term>, and that we may thereby be made fit for the virtue to which his teaching points us, and in which his example becomes our pattern. Then we will grow in the expansion and enlightenment <note>The German "Aufklärung" here refers to the spiritual and intellectual clarification of one’s understanding through divine grace.</note> of our abilities and concepts; our hearts will be more receptive to the good, and from this, the blessed fruit of Jesus's teaching will be recognized, as our heart is continually further cultivated and improved through it. The second part of these reflections sheds more light on this.

---

Christ, who was himself human—and thus knew even the slightest weaknesses and temptations of human nature—who himself had to experience, in the highest degree and most sensitively, the unpleasantness of the one and the laborious struggle with the other, must have truly felt the pitiable misery of mankind when he lovingly called out to them:

...called out: Come to me, all you who are weary and burdened, I will refresh you. The sufferings of the soul were known to him, the Merciful One himself, as being far more elevated and unbearable than those of wounded sensuality <term>Sinnlichkeit: In this context, it refers to physical sensations and the "lower" worldly impulses of the body.</term>. For even on the stem of the cross <note>original: *Creuzesstamme*. A poetic reference to the wooden crucifix.</note> he did not waver, but with love He always looked down upon others more than upon himself, and amidst the most bitter sufferings he was still always mindful of the salvation of his own—even of his tormentors. But at Gethsemane, where he felt the whole burden of the sins and the misery of vice of the human race upon himself, there he began—as Scripture teaches—to tremble and to despair. There, where the wrath of the Godhead that dwelt bodily within him made itself so emphatically felt by his human nature, and he cried out to the same: Why have you forsaken me? <note>original: "Warum hast du mich verlassen." Though famously spoken on the Cross, the author associates this feeling of divine abandonment with the spiritual weight felt during the Agony in the Garden.</note>; there, friends, there he, the divine Merciful One, felt the full weight of the sins which must necessarily weigh upon a heart devoted to God and bring about repentance and shame over the vices committed. Therefore, even beforehand, his teachings were always intent on commending himself to the fallen sinner as the only means of salvation, and on creating a receptivity in their hearts to receive him and his word therein. —

Before the arrival and appearance of Jesus, the Jewish people bound themselves only in a quite slavish manner, as it were, to the letter of the law; through Jesus’s teachings

however, we were only convinced that God is not a God of zeal <note>original: *Eifer*. In this context, it refers to the biblical concept of divine jealousy or "holy zeal" often associated with judgment.</note> and wrath, but rather of love, and that this alone is what counts with Him; thus, our hearts were encouraged toward love through the teachings of Jesus, while all hardness, all vengefulness, enmity, and all judging of one's neighbor were banished therefrom. Christ wishes all fear of punishment to be banished as unbefitting human nature, and in all His teachings He aims even more at good sentiments than at outward actions; He presents virtues solely as being of service and utility to mankind, and requires that in the arduous struggle, one should take refuge in His powerful divine support so that these <note>The virtues.</note> may nonetheless reign within. — All of this was bound to strip away what was crude and wild from the heart, and instill in it an attachment to God through Christ—to that God whom it previously knew only as a strict judge, but through Jesus, came to know as the highest Love. — It calls out to us, this source of all love and benevolence:

### Dearest Brothers!

Before we end our work <note>In a fraternal or Masonic context, "work" refers to the ritual proceedings and the collective moral labor of the group.</note>: let us lift our hearts to God, and in our prayer be mindful of all those who wander in want and tribulation, in sickness and misery, and to whom only the teaching of Jesus and the sure hope of the imputation <note>original: "Zurechnung"; a theological concept where the divine righteousness or "merit" of Christ is credited to the believer's account.</note> of his divine merit grants comfort on the bitter path of their lives. We also especially remember in our prayer Her *), whom our hearts, so entirely devoted to her, most inwardly revere, and who knows how to win the hearts of all through deeds of kindness of every kind, but who is now visited by the Lord with a painful illness, so that she may powerfully bear witness even here to the composure and patience of a true Christian woman. — We beseech you, O Lord, you who always look down with mercy upon the people who are permitted to call you Friend and Brother, look now also upon the misery of humanity, upon all their tribulations and want, and deliver them; yet even more, O Lord!

<margin>*) Her Serene Highness the Reigning Duchess of O. in S. <note>original: "Ihro Durchlaucht die Regierende Frau Herzogin zu O. in S." This likely refers to a local noble patroness of the order or region.</note></margin>

# Dearest, most inwardly beloved brothers!

When a person casts a sincere glance at his own nature, he finds within it—no matter how good his will may be, or how much it is directed toward the promotion of his true bliss—a contradiction caused by the connection with his <term>sensuality</term> <note>The German word *Sinnlichkeit* here refers to the physical senses and worldly inclinations which, in 18th-century thought, were often seen as being in conflict with the rational or spiritual "will."</note>, which places a constant obstacle in his path. In the first case, the cause of the temptation is solely within us and could be called **inner temptation**; in the second case, since the cause lies in the object located outside of us, it would be **outer temptation**; in the third, however, since the causes are twofold, one might call it **mixed temptation**. Although the inadequacy and the wretched weakness of mockery is already sufficiently clear to every rational person, it nevertheless happens quite often that its outer shell is very brilliant in wit <note>German: *Wiz* (modern: *Witz*). Mixed temptations <term>Anfechtungen; literally "assaults" or "trials," referring here to the moral struggles or "temptations" an individual faces</term> are produced by the allure of an object existing outside of us that acts upon us, and by an internal inclination toward the enjoyment of or participation in this object. Dearest friends! I am certain that all the former temptations, should they sprout even for a moment in your hearts, would be immediately suppressed; but if ever I have wished for eloquence, it is here in this moment, so that I might depict for you in the most natural colors the allure, the danger, and the inevitable ruin caused by lust <term>Wollust; in this 18th-century context, this term refers to sensual or sexual desire, often framed as a "storm" of the senses that overwhelms reason</term>.

<meta>This page continues the moral exhortation regarding the struggle between Reason and Lust. It completes the sentence from the previous page concerning the "occurrence" of a moral fall and expands on the physical and spiritual degradation that follows repeated vice.</meta>

...rence <note>The text begins with a fragment "-rung," completing the word "Vorfahrung" (occurrence/experience) from the previous page.</note>; but, as unfortunately human nature <term>menschliche Natur; the inherent physical and moral makeup of human beings, often viewed in 18th-century philosophy as being in a state of tension between higher aspirations and lower instincts</term> is of such a quality that a first mistake commonly becomes the first step toward a following series of similar offenses, such a person falls even more often; and then, friends! use your imagination <term>Einbildungskraft; the creative or visual power of the mind to contemplate things not present</term> for a moment, to contemplate a body wasted by disease and misery, which is only still capable of asserting its superiority over the beast through an imperfect speech at most, and no longer through reason <term>Vernunft; the rational faculty considered the highest human attribute</term> and mental faculties <term>Geistesfähigkeiten; the intellectual and spiritual powers of the soul</term>, which are entirely suppressed in him. 

And imagine this being in connection with an eternity <term>Ewigkeit; the timeless state of the soul after death</term>, where he is meant to further develop his mental faculties and, as the reward of grace <term>Gnadenlohn; a theological concept of a reward granted by God's mercy rather than strictly earned by human merit</term> for a genuine virtuous life <term>Tugendwandel; a consistent path of moral and ethical conduct</term> in faith in Him <note>A reference to Jesus Christ</note>, whose merit has obtained for him access to the throne of God, he is to attain a closer knowledge of the divine Being. Imagine this person in that eternity, where everything is lacking that once caused him satisfaction, and having now crossed over into a field where he is supposed to use his higher faculty, with whose powers he has remained completely unacquainted.
