<meta>This page continues the speaker’s moral exhortation, shifting from the description of external and internal dangers to the practical remedies required to overcome them. It emphasizes the limitations of human will and the necessity of divine aid through prayer.</meta>

The means to properly resist temptations <term>Anfechtungen; spiritual trials or moral "assaults" that test a person's character</term> must be determined by the nature of these trials and by the nature of man. The former call us to a constant vigilance—to always keep before our eyes the dangers that surround us at all times, to avoid them, and where this is not possible, to apply the means to resist and combat them, which must be appropriate to our purpose and our nature. If we look at our own nature for this end, we must necessarily become convinced of the inadequacy of our own powers to bring about good. A constant remembrance of this weakness, combined with a vivid mental image of the dangers awaiting and surrounding us, and with humble submission to the higher, supporting power of the Most High <note>original: "Höchsten"; a common reverent title for God</note>, expressed in a devout prayer pleasing to God—one also recommended to us by Christ in the words of our text—seems to me the most effective and appropriate means against temptations of all kinds. — It seems necessary to me to illuminate these means even more closely. — The more we become acquainted with ourselves, the more we are convinced of the overwhelming power of sensuality <term>Sinnlichkeit; the physical senses and lower appetites which, in the philosophy of the time, were often seen as being in conflict with the rational or spiritual will</term> over our higher powers and abilities; and the sooner we experience how our best intentions remain fruitless because we cannot use our suppressed forces, therefore

<summary>This page explores the psychological and spiritual strategies for resisting moral trials, specifically highlighting the necessity of self-awareness, vigilance, and prayer. through obedience bears witness to his purest gratitude, promotes his own bliss <term>Glückseligkeit: a central Enlightenment concept referring to a state of lasting, spiritual happiness and well-being</term>, and thereby achieves the purpose of his true destiny, which can only consist in the glorification of the great God and in the attainment of a genuine contentment appropriate to him. --- Now, just as necessary as obedience to the laws which the Most High has placed within his [man's] own nature, and which the other works of creation proclaim to man, must be linked to his bliss, so too does all disobedience against the divine precepts conflict with it, and entirely hinders the glorification of the Creator, which I may rightly call the greatest bliss of man, since it leads to the knowledge of God and His works, the consequence of which can only be a noble proficiency in virtue. <term>Tugendfertigkeit: the habitual and practiced ability to act virtuously; a key goal in Masonic self-improvement.</term>

Here, thanks be to You, O wise and kind Providence! that You did not desire the total destruction of the fallen sinner, but that You willed he should turn back to You and live, and that through the ways of Your sublime wisdom, known only to You, You chose the necessary punishment for the transgression—death—as the sole means for his salvation and bliss <term>Beseligung: the state of attaining spiritual happiness or eternal salvation</term>, as Your holy messenger <note>A reference to Christ, framed here as the "holy messenger" who bridges the gap between the divine and the physical</note> united himself most intimately with human nature to cleanse it from the curse of sins, and through his death to bring life and an imperishable nature to light. The gift of God, Reason, appointed as the guide for man on the path of this life—a life interwoven with many temptations that often seem so extremely sweet to mortals—has often endeavored to prove to the person who knows of no revelation, from the attributes of the Highest Being and from his own nature, the certainty of the immortality of his higher spiritual faculty. <meta>This page continues the argument that while human reason and ancient traditions were uncertain about the afterlife, the arrival of Christ provided definitive proof and transformed the meaning of death from a punishment into a process of purification.</meta>

...to those abandoned to the **light of nature** <term>Lumen Naturae: the human capacity for reason and logic without the help of supernatural revelation</term> and the guidance of their own reason, it was entirely unknown. But this was now to be made known to the entire world through the **appearance of Christ**, the messenger of God.

Just as death had come upon the whole world through one man <note>A reference to Adam; the text follows the Pauline theology of Romans 5, contrasting the fall of Adam with the redemption of Christ.</note>, so too should the **Godhead**—most closely united with the human nature of Christ—bring life and **resurrection** to light once more through the **imputation of his merit** <note>The theological concept that Christ's perfect life and sacrifice are credited to humanity.</note>. He, Christ, was to die and rise again as a man, serving as our **prototype** <note>original: "Fürbilde"</note>, so that we might see through him that God's **grace** will also awaken us.

He, our divine predecessor, stripped death of its annihilating power and transformed it into a true **purification** and **separation** of all the coarse and earthly-sensual parts that hinder us from the authentic performance of actions pleasing to God. This serves to make our "tools" <note>Likely referring to the physical body or the lower faculties of the soul.</note> fit for the service of the spirit directed toward God. Now death—which for us was terror and horror, the enemy of our being—is the wholesome means of our happiness, the sure mark of an unmistakable grace of a merciful God, despite being a consequence of our transgressions. --- Page 342 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical and theological reflection on the limitations of physical existence. The author expands on the idea that our "sensuality" (bodily nature) acts as a hindrance to both intellectual pursuits and spiritual devotion, framing death and physical decay as necessary processes of liberation and refinement.</meta>

...also produce the very wish to see the **sensual** <term>Sinnliche: the physical or material aspects of human existence, pertaining to the senses</term> abolished, in order to be able to act more freely and unhindered, and to remain peaceful without suffering. How often is the noble gift of **Reason** <term>Vernunft: in Enlightenment thought, the faculty that allows humans to understand the world and moral truths</term> not entirely suppressed by sensuality! 

Consider a person weakened by illness, who feels within themselves the liveliest desire to equip themselves with knowledge, but whose **coarse tool** <note>The "tool" (Werkzeug) is a common Enlightenment metaphor for the physical body, which serves the "master" or the spirit.</note> and its weakness do not permit it; who is thus forced to lead a life that is entirely contrary to their inclination and satisfaction. How welcome to such a person is death, which frees him from that which was an obstacle to his **bliss** <note>original: "Glückseligkeit"; a state of ultimate spiritual fulfillment and well-being</note>, and now grants him the opportunity to follow his inclination and to use his spiritual faculties unhindered. 

Even more sorrowful is the case of one who, with a heart directed entirely toward God, strives to put into practice what is pleasing to God, but who—through temptations and trials of all kinds that are closely connected to his sensuality—is constantly disturbed, if not entirely prevented, in almost all his efforts; who must thus spend his life in a torturous struggle. Death abolishes all of this. **Decay** <term>Verwesung: here used not just as biological rot, but as a transformative "cleansing agent" that breaks down the flawed physical form</term>, this universal cleansing agent of nature, becomes such for man as well; his shell, his tool, thereby becomes more suited to the will of his spirit, and then

<summary>This page argues that death is a welcome release from the "coarse tool" of the physical body, which often hinders human reason and spiritual devotion. It frames physical decay as a natural purification process that prepares the spirit for a higher state of activity.</summary>
<keywords>sensuality, reason, illness, death, bliss, spiritual struggle, decay, nature, spirit</keywords>

then he can act according to his abilities, undisturbed, without being exposed to either suffering or temptation. From this, the **benefit** <term>Wohlthat: a kind act or blessing</term> of death in our current state is sufficiently clear; it serves as a convincing proof of the benevolent intentions of the **Most High** <note>A common Enlightenment and pietist way of referring to God, emphasizing His supreme sovereignty.</note>, in that He has transformed this fitting avenger of our transgressions in such a singular way into the very entrance to our **bliss** <term>Glückseligkeit: a state of perfect, eternal happiness or spiritual fulfillment</term>. 

— If we further consider death—according to the **imputation** <term>Zurechnung: a theological concept where the righteousness or "merit" of Christ is credited to a person's account</term> of the divine merit of Christ—as the means to glorify the great Creator with far purer spiritual powers within a much more extensive sphere of action, and as the entrance to a much greater bliss than man could have expected according to his own inherent nature, then death surely appears to everyone as a very special **grace of God**, and one might rightly call it the greatest testimony of grace from the Most High. 

— How shall we, dearest friends, offer the thanks we owe for this to the Most High? Must not shame and repentance seize us at the same time?

They are, therefore, already unhappy within themselves at the moment of death and must fear it. Even if a certain dizzying deception of the senses takes place in some people—through which they seem courageous in all dangers and even appear to face death without fear—this only proves that all sensitivity of the **conscience** <term>Gewissen: the internal moral sense of right and wrong</term> has already been suppressed; it by no means proves that this apparent composure is natural. Even the finest and most pleasant feelings that allowed the wise man to walk his path with pleasure here in this **earthly life**, the feelings of friendship, the love of spouses for one another, of parents for their children, and of children for their parents—these feelings, which give life its entire value and pleasantness—are suspended by death, at least for a time. Thus, despite all the conceptions of the great advantages that grow for us out of death, and which so undeniably bear witness to the **grace of God** <term>Gnade Gottes: the unmerited favor and divine help given by God</term>, it still requires an **overcoming** <note>Original: "Ueberwindung." Although the **spiritual man** <term>Geistmensch: the inner, rational soul or the intellect guided by divine spirit, as opposed to physical impulses.</term> must always rule over it, and it should never step outside its bounds, it must nevertheless currently contribute to the achievement of our noble purpose: to glorify the great **Creator**. Since it is only through the maintenance of sensuality that spiritual faculties are kept in their effectiveness, it becomes a duty to care for this as well, and to be mindful that any burgeoning uprising of the senses over our higher capacity be suppressed. The more the strength and power of the spiritual man attains proficiency, the lesser and less burdensome the struggle with that sensuality becomes; the purer and more unblemished our **virtuous conduct**, the more calmed our **conscience**, and with the greater pleasure we look forward to the moment of our **dissolution** <note>Auflösung: literally "dissolving," a common historical term for death, describing the soul's release from the physical body.</note>, and the more easily we overcome the natur[al]—
